## D-10.1 Continuous Security Monitoring

> **Provenance.** Merged pipeline: CrossRegulation/DeepAnalysis (§1) + HierarchicalSecurityObjectives (§2) + SecurityRequirements (§3). All content preserved VERBATIM from sources; only paths were normalized (../../../ → ../../).

### 1. Cross-Regulation Analysis


> **Domain analysis:** [D-10.1 Continuous Security Monitoring](../../DomainAnalysis/D-09_Governance-Documentation/D-10.1.md)

#### D-10.1 Continuous Security Monitoring

<!-- participants: GDPR, NIS2, CRA, DORA, AI_Act -->

**Participants (from CRDA):** GDPR, NIS2, CRA, DORA, AI_Act

**Critical note (CRDA flag — Layer 2 review):** The CRA user opt-out (Annex I Part I (2)(l)) and the GDPR mandatory monitoring (Art. 32(2)) conflict for a CRA-regulated product processing personal data. The OJ does not resolve this conflict. Recommend Layer 2 review.

##### Pair: GDPR ↔ NIS2

**GDPR article (verbatim, Art. 32(2) + Art. 33(1)):**
> "Taking into account the state of the art, the cost of implementation and the nature, scope, context and purposes of processing, as well as the risks of varying likelihood and severity for the rights and freedoms of natural persons, the controller and the processor shall implement appropriate technical and organisational measures … such as … (b) the ability to ensure the ongoing confidentiality, integrity, availability and resilience of processing systems and services; (c) the ability to restore the availability and access to personal data in a timely manner … (d) a process for regularly testing, assessing and evaluating the effectiveness of technical and organisational measures …" — Art. 32(1)+(2) GDPR

**NIS2 article (verbatim, Art. 21(2)(b) detection dimension):**
> "[Member States shall ensure that essential and important entities take appropriate and proportionate technical, operational and organisational measures to … ] (b) incident handling …" — Art. 21(2)(b) NIS 2 (per SR-NIS2-009)

**Scope comparison:**
- GDPR scope: personal-data processing systems monitored for adverse events covering 5 Art. 32(2) risk types; breach-`becoming-aware` anchoring for Art. 33(1) notification clock.
- NIS 2 scope: all-hazards monitoring to surface adverse events (incident-handling detection capability).
- Scope overlap: **Y (when the controller-NSIE processes personal data through NIS — detection capability overlaps).**

**Trigger comparison:**
- GDPR trigger: 5 Art. 32(2) risk types (accidental/unlawful destruction, loss, alteration, unauthorised disclosure/access).
- NIS 2 trigger: incident-handling detection capability (all-hazards).
- Trigger alignment: **PARTIAL** (different triggering frameworks — risk-type-triggered vs. incident-triggered).

**Threshold/timeline comparison:**
- GDPR: breach-`becoming-aware` anchoring for Art. 33(1) 72-hour notification clock.
- NIS 2: no quantitative threshold for monitoring.
- Tension: **NONE**.

**Recipient comparison:**
- GDPR: data subject, DPA.
- NIS 2: management body, competent authority.

**Content template comparison:**
- GDPR: not codified for content.
- NIS 2: not codified for content (incident-handling 6-action lifecycle per SR-NIS2-008).

**Classified relationship (from CRDA):** COMPLEMENTARY
**Verified relationship (after OJ-text analysis):** **SAME — COMPLEMENTARY**
**Reasoning:** GDPR monitoring anchors on 5 risk types; NIS 2 monitoring anchors on incident-handling detection capability. The two regimes cover overlapping detection capability but with different triggering frameworks (risk-type-triggered vs. incident-triggered).

**Scope-disjoint test:** Does this pair actually overlap on a single event at a single party? **Y (when the controller-NSIE processes personal data through NIS)**. No reclassification required.

**Downstream implication (Phase 1C Doc 07):** Phase 2 obligation derivation must produce a layered detection stack: GDPR Art. 32(2) 5-risk-type monitoring + NIS 2 Art. 21(2)(b) incident-handling detection capability.

---

##### Pair: GDPR ↔ CRA

**GDPR article (verbatim, Art. 32(2) + Art. 33(1)):**
> [as quoted above]

**CRA article (verbatim, Annex I Part I (2)(l) + Part II (6)):**
> "On the basis of the cybersecurity risk assessment referred to in Article 13(2) … the product with digital elements shall: … (l) provide security-related information by recording and monitoring relevant internal activity, including access to or modification of data, services or functions, and provide an opt-out mechanism for the user." — CRA Annex I Part I (2)(l) (per SR-CRA-100)
> "The manufacturer shall … (6) facilitate the sharing of information about potential vulnerabilities of the product with digital elements …" — CRA Annex I Part II (6)

**Scope comparison:**
- GDPR scope: entity-level monitoring (5 Art. 32(2) risk types).
- CRA scope: product-level on-device activity monitoring with user opt-out + vulnerability-reporting contact address.
- Scope overlap: **Y (when the CRA-regulated product processes personal data)**.

**Trigger comparison:**
- GDPR trigger: 5 Art. 32(2) risk types.
- CRA trigger: access/modification of data/services/functions + vulnerability reporting.
- Trigger alignment: **PARTIAL**.

**Threshold/timeline comparison:**
- GDPR: breach-`becoming-aware` 72h clock.
- CRA: support-period obligation.
- Tension: **GENUINE (CRA user opt-out vs. GDPR mandatory monitoring — see CRDA Layer 2 review flag)**.

**Recipient comparison:**
- GDPR: data subject, DPA.
- CRA: MSA, users.

**Content template comparison:**
- GDPR: not codified for content.
- CRA: Annex VII §6 test reports + Annex VII §2(b) CVD policy.

**Classified relationship (from CRDA):** COMPLEMENTARY
**Verified relationship (after OJ-text analysis):** **SAME — COMPLEMENTARY** (with CRDA-flagged Layer 2 conflict on user opt-out vs. mandatory monitoring)
**Reasoning:** GDPR monitoring is entity-level; CRA monitoring is product-level with user opt-out. The two regimes meet at the controller-manufacturer interface. **CRITICAL CONFLICT (Layer 2 review):** CRA Annex I Part I (2)(l) allows user opt-out from on-device activity monitoring; GDPR Art. 32(2) monitoring is mandatory for the 5 risk types; DORA Art. 8(1) monitoring is mandatory. For a CRA-regulated product processing personal data, the CRA user opt-out conflicts with the GDPR mandatory monitoring. The OJ does not resolve this conflict.

**Scope-disjoint test:** Does this pair actually overlap on a single event at a single party? **Y (when the CRA-regulated product processes personal data)**. No reclassification required.

**Downstream implication (Phase 1C Doc 07):** Phase 2 obligation derivation must surface the CRA user opt-out vs. GDPR mandatory monitoring conflict for Layer 2 review. The two regimes can stack, but the user's opt-out from CRA monitoring may not legally exempt the controller from GDPR Art. 32(2) mandatory detection — the conflict requires OJ-level resolution.

---

##### Pair: GDPR ↔ DORA

**GDPR article (verbatim, Art. 32(2) + Art. 33(1)):**
> [as quoted above]

**DORA article (verbatim, Art. 8(1) per SR-DORA-030; OJ-literal locus Art. 9(1) per the v0.2 audit):**
> "Financial entities shall continuously monitor and control the security and functioning of ICT systems and tools and shall minimise the impact of ICT risk on ICT systems through the deployment of appropriate ICT security tools, policies and procedures." — DORA Art. 8(1) per SR-DORA-030 (OJ-literal locus Art. 9(1))

**Scope comparison:**
- GDPR scope: entity-level monitoring (5 Art. 32(2) risk types).
- DORA scope: financial-entity continuous monitoring and control of ICT-system security and functioning (CIA+A).
- Scope overlap: **Y (when the DORA financial entity is a controller processing personal data)**.

**Trigger comparison:**
- GDPR trigger: 5 Art. 32(2) risk types.
- DORA trigger: ICT-system security and functioning monitoring (CIA+A).
- Trigger alignment: **PARTIAL** (DORA is broader — security AND functioning; GDPR is 5 risk types).

**Threshold/timeline comparison:**
- GDPR: breach-`becoming-aware` 72h clock.
- DORA: continuous (Art. 9(1)).
- Tension: **NONE**.

**Recipient comparison:**
- GDPR: data subject, DPA.
- DORA: NCA.

**Content template comparison:**
- GDPR: not codified for content.
- DORA: monitoring tools + policies + procedures.

**Classified relationship (from CRDA):** COMPLEMENTARY
**Verified relationship (after OJ-text analysis):** **SAME — COMPLEMENTARY**
**Reasoning:** DORA monitoring is broader (security and functioning) than GDPR monitoring (5 risk types). DORA subsumes the GDPR detection capability within its entity-level continuous-monitoring mandate for financial entities.

**Scope-disjoint test:** Does this pair actually overlap on a single event at a single party? **Y (when the DORA financial entity is a controller processing personal data)**. No reclassification required.

**Downstream implication (Phase 1C Doc 07):** Phase 2 obligation derivation must produce a DORA-led stack: DORA Art. 9(1) continuous monitoring + GDPR Art. 32(2) 5-risk-type detection as a sub-component.

---

##### Pair: GDPR ↔ AI_Act

**GDPR article (verbatim, Art. 32(2) + Art. 33(1)):**
> [as quoted above]

**AI_Act article (verbatim, Art. 72(1)–(2)):**
> "Providers shall establish and document a post-market monitoring system in a manner that is proportionate to the nature of the AI technologies and the risks of the high-risk AI system. The post-market monitoring system shall actively and systematically collect, document and analyse relevant data …" — Art. 72(1)–(2) AI Act (per SR-AIACT-021)

**Scope comparison:**
- GDPR scope: pre-breach-detection monitoring (5 Art. 32(2) risk types).
- AI Act scope: post-market monitoring of high-risk AI systems (lifecycle monitoring).
- Scope overlap: **Conditional (Y when a high-risk AI system processes personal data — provider is controller)**.

**Trigger comparison:**
- GDPR trigger: 5 Art. 32(2) risk types.
- AI Act trigger: high-risk AI system post-market data.
- Trigger alignment: **DIFFERENT** (pre-breach vs. lifecycle).

**Threshold/timeline comparison:**
- GDPR: breach-`becoming-aware` 72h clock.
- AI Act: `proportionate to the nature of the AI technologies and the risks` — qualitative.
- Tension: **NONE**.

**Recipient comparison:**
- GDPR: data subject, DPA.
- AI Act: provider, MSA.

**Content template comparison:**
- GDPR: not codified for content.
- AI Act: post-market monitoring plan + data collection and analysis.

**Classified relationship (from CRDA):** COMPLEMENTARY
**Verified relationship (after OJ-text analysis):** **SAME — COMPLEMENTARY**
**Reasoning:** GDPR monitoring is pre-breach-detection; AI Act post-market monitoring is lifecycle monitoring of high-risk AI systems. The two regimes stack.

**Scope-disjoint test:** Does this pair actually overlap on a single event at a single party? **Conditional (Y when the high-risk AI system processes personal data)**. No reclassification required.

**Downstream implication (Phase 1C Doc 07):** Phase 2 obligation derivation must produce a layered stack: GDPR Art. 32(2) pre-breach detection + AI Act Art. 72 post-market monitoring.

> [as quoted above]

##### Pair: NIS2 ↔ DORA

**NIS2 article (verbatim, Art. 21(2)(b)):**
> [as quoted above]

**DORA article (verbatim, Art. 9(1)):**
> [as quoted in GDPR ↔ DORA above]

**Scope comparison:**
- NIS 2 scope: all-hazards monitoring (partial coverage via incident-handling detection dimension).
- DORA scope: financial-entity continuous monitoring and control.
- Scope overlap: **Y (when the DORA financial entity is also an NSIE)**.

**Trigger comparison:**
- NIS 2 trigger: all-hazards monitoring.
- DORA trigger: ICT-system security and functioning monitoring (CIA+A).
- Trigger alignment: **SAME** (both continuous monitoring).

**Threshold/timeline comparison:**
- NIS 2: continuous.
- DORA: continuous (Art. 9(1)).
- Tension: **NONE**.

**Recipient comparison:**
- NIS 2: management body, competent authority.
- DORA: NCA.

**Content template comparison:**
- NIS 2: not codified for content.

**Reasoning:** Both regulations impose continuous-monitoring obligations. DORA Art. 9(1) is the dominant authority for financial entities with explicit OJ-literal anchor.

### 2. Hierarchical Security Objective


## D-10.1 Continuous Security Monitoring

> **CRDA-deep provenance.** This section was generated from
> [`../../CrossRegulation/DeepAnalysis/D-09_Governance-Documentation/D-10.1.md`](../../CrossRegulation/DeepAnalysis/D-09_Governance-Documentation/D-10.1.md)
> (10 pairs verified against OJ text — **5 participating regulations
> are GDPR, NIS 2, CRA, DORA, AI Act; all 5 participate** as noted by
> `DomainAnalysis/D-09_Governance-Documentation/D-10.1.md` line 27:
> "Participants: GDPR, NIS2, CRA, DORA, AI_Act").
>
> **Critical note (CRDA flag — Layer 2 review required).** D-10.1
> surfaces **1 CRDA-flagged genuine tension**: the CRA user opt-out
> (Annex I Part I (2)(l)) conflicts with the GDPR mandatory monitoring
> (Art. 32(2)) for a CRA-regulated product processing personal data.
> The OJ does not resolve this conflict. The same CRA-opt-out flag
> propagates to the NIS 2 ↔ CRA and CRA ↔ DORA pairs (NIS 2 Art.
> 21(2)(b) mandatory monitoring vs. CRA opt-out; DORA Art. 8(1)
> mandatory monitoring vs. CRA opt-out). Three pairs carry the same
> GENUINE TENSION flag (GDPR↔CRA, NIS2↔CRA, CRA↔DORA) — the conflict
> is structurally between the CRA Annex I Part I (2)(l) opt-out and the
> three mandatory-monitoring regimes. The high-level SO does NOT
> collapse this tension; it is preserved as a conditional emergent
> tension requiring Layer 2 OJ-level resolution.

#### Participants (from CRDA-deep §D-10.1)

| Regulation | Canonical SO | 1-line summary | Scope/angle |
|---|---|---|---|
| GDPR | SO-GDPR-030 + SO-GDPR-008 | Personal-data processing systems and services are monitored for adverse events covering the five Art. 32(2) risk types (accidental or unlawful destruction, loss, alteration, unauthorised disclosure of, or access to personal data), with a clear definition of the moment of "becoming aware of" a breach to anchor the Art. 33(1) 72-hour notification clock. | Entity-level monitoring of personal-data processing systems; 5-risk-type trigger (Art. 32(2) chapeau `a process for regularly testing, assessing and evaluating`); `becoming-aware` clock for breach notification. |
| NIS2 | SO-NIS2-005 (partial) | An incident-handling capability is established, covering the prevention, detection, analysis, containment, response, and recovery of incidents, including those with significant impact on the provision of services (Art. 21(2)(b) — incident handling). | All-hazards detection capability as a partial-coverage dimension of the incident-handling obligation; no dedicated continuous-monitoring mandate; D-10.1 partial coverage. |
| CRA | SO-CRA-020 + SO-CRA-068 | Relevant internal activity of the product with digital elements — including access to or modification of data, services, or functions — is recorded and monitored, with a user-visible opt-out mechanism where appropriate; manufacturer enables continuous security-relevant internal-activity monitoring such that security-relevant events are observable by the manufacturer and (where appropriate) reportable to the user (Annex I Part I (2)(l) + Annex I Part II (6)). | Product-level on-device internal-activity monitoring with **user opt-out** + vulnerability-reporting mechanism; manufacturer-side observability. |
| DORA | SO-DORA-018 + SO-DORA-007 + SO-DORA-003 | ICT systems and tools are continuously monitored and controlled for security and functioning (Art. 8(1) per `DORA-C07` — OJ-literal locus Art. 9(1)) + anomalous activities promptly detected through multi-layered controls (Art. 10(1)+(2)) + ICT risk identified on a continuous basis (Art. 7(2)). | Financial-entity continuous monitoring + control of ICT-system security and functioning (CIA+A); prompt detection of anomalous activities; continuous ICT risk identification. |
| AI_Act | SO-AIACT-012 | A post-market monitoring system is established and documented in a manner that is proportionate to the nature of the AI technologies and the risks of the high-risk AI system, actively and systematically collecting, documenting and analysing relevant data to evaluate continued compliance; market-surveillance cooperation under Regulation (EU) 2019/1020 applies (Art. 72(1)–(2) + Art. 74(1)). | AI-system post-market monitoring (lifecycle monitoring of high-risk AI); `proportionate to the nature of the AI technologies and the risks` (qualitative); `actively and systematically collect, document and analyse` (3-way verb AND). |

#### Validated relationships (from CRDA-deep §D-10.1)

Of 10 pairs, **10 verified as SAME** (no CORRECTED reclassifications). The 10 verified relationships are distributed as **9 SAME — COMPLEMENTARY + 1 SAME — EQUAL (NIS 2 ↔ DORA)**. **1 CRDA-flagged GENUINE TENSION** (GDPR↔CRA — CRA user opt-out vs. GDPR mandatory monitoring; CRDA-flagged for Layer 2 review; same flag propagates to NIS2↔CRA and CRA↔DORA pairs but the structural conflict originates at the CRA opt-out clause). **All 10 pairs pass scope-disjoint test** (Y or Conditional-Y-on-same-party — no N (typically) pairs). The five regulations look at the continuous-monitoring duty from five distinct regulatory perspectives that stack with one genuine tension to resolve at the OJ level:

- **GDPR ↔ NIS2** — COMPLEMENTARY (SAME): GDPR monitoring anchors on 5 Art. 32(2) risk types; NIS 2 monitoring anchors on incident-handling detection capability (all-hazards). The two regimes cover overlapping detection capability but with different triggering frameworks (risk-type-triggered vs. incident-triggered). Trigger alignment PARTIAL; threshold tension NONE (breach-`becoming-aware` 72h vs. no quantitative threshold); recipient GDPR data subject + DPA vs. NIS 2 management body + competent authority. Scope-disjoint test: **Y (when the controller-NSIE processes personal data through NIS)**. No reclassification required.
- **GDPR ↔ CRA** — COMPLEMENTARY (SAME) — **CRDA-flagged GENUINE TENSION (Layer 2 review required)**: GDPR monitoring is entity-level (5 Art. 32(2) risk types); CRA monitoring is product-level on-device activity monitoring with **user opt-out**. The two regimes meet at the controller-manufacturer interface. **CRITICAL CONFLICT:** CRA Annex I Part I (2)(l) allows user opt-out from on-device activity monitoring; GDPR Art. 32(2) monitoring is mandatory for the 5 risk types; DORA Art. 8(1) monitoring is mandatory. For a CRA-regulated product processing personal data, the CRA user opt-out conflicts with the GDPR mandatory monitoring. **The OJ does not resolve this conflict.** The high-level SO does NOT collapse the tension — the conflict is preserved as a conditional emergent tension requiring Layer 2 OJ-level review. Scope-disjoint test: **Y (when the CRA-regulated product processes personal data)**. No reclassification required on the relationship itself (still COMPLEMENTARY), but the conflict remains flagged.
- **GDPR ↔ DORA** — COMPLEMENTARY (SAME): DORA monitoring is broader (security AND functioning) than GDPR monitoring (5 risk types). DORA Art. 8(1) per `SR-DORA-030` (OJ-literal locus Art. 9(1)) subsumes the GDPR detection capability within its entity-level continuous-monitoring mandate for financial entities. Trigger alignment PARTIAL (DORA broader); threshold tension NONE (GDPR 72h vs. DORA continuous); recipient GDPR data subject + DPA vs. DORA NCA. Scope-disjoint test: **Y (when the DORA financial entity is a controller processing personal data)**. No reclassification required.
- **GDPR ↔ AI_Act** — COMPLEMENTARY (SAME): GDPR monitoring is pre-breach-detection (5 Art. 32(2) risk types); AI Act post-market monitoring is lifecycle monitoring of high-risk AI systems (`actively and systematically collect, document and analyse` per Art. 72(1)–(2)). Trigger alignment DIFFERENT (pre-breach vs. lifecycle); threshold tension NONE (GDPR 72h vs. AI Act qualitative proportionality); recipient GDPR data subject + DPA vs. AI Act provider + MSA. Scope-disjoint test: **Conditional (Y when a high-risk AI system processes personal data — provider is controller)**. No reclassification required.
- **NIS2 ↔ CRA** — COMPLEMENTARY (SAME) — **CRDA-flagged GENUINE TENSION (Layer 2 review required, propagated from GDPR↔CRA)**: NIS 2 monitoring is entity-level (all-hazards detection capability per Art. 21(2)(b)); CRA monitoring is product-level on-device activity with **user opt-out**. The two regimes meet at the supplier-customer interface. **Tension flag (same as GDPR↔CRA):** CRA user opt-out vs. NIS 2 mandatory monitoring — the structural conflict originates at the CRA Annex I Part I (2)(l) opt-out clause and propagates to any mandatory-monitoring regime. Scope-disjoint test: **Conditional (Y when the NSIE deploys CRA-regulated products)**. No reclassification required on the relationship itself (still COMPLEMENTARY), but the conflict remains flagged.
- **NIS2 ↔ DORA** — EQUAL (SAME): Both regulations impose continuous-monitoring obligations. NIS 2 Art. 21(2)(b) detection capability vs. DORA Art. 8(1) `continuously monitor and control the security and functioning of ICT systems and tools` per `SR-DORA-030` (OJ-literal locus Art. 9(1)). Trigger alignment SAME (both continuous monitoring). DORA Art. 9(1) is the dominant authority for financial entities with explicit OJ-literal anchor. Scope-disjoint test: **Y (when the DORA financial entity is also an NSIE applying continuous monitoring)**. No reclassification required.
- **NIS2 ↔ AI_Act** — COMPLEMENTARY (SAME): NIS 2 monitoring is entity-level operational; AI Act post-market monitoring is AI-system-level lifecycle. Trigger alignment DIFFERENT; threshold tension NONE (NIS 2 continuous vs. AI Act qualitative proportionality). The two regimes stack without overlap. Scope-disjoint test: **Conditional (Y when the NSIE deploys high-risk AI)**. No reclassification required.
- **CRA ↔ DORA** — COMPLEMENTARY (SAME) — **CRDA-flagged GENUINE TENSION (Layer 2 review required, propagated from GDPR↔CRA)**: CRA product-level on-device monitoring with **user opt-out** feeds DORA entity-level continuous monitoring (Art. 8(1)). Trigger alignment DIFFERENT (CRA access/modification of data/services/functions vs. DORA ICT-system security and functioning); threshold tension NONE (CRA support-period vs. DORA continuous); **tension flag (same as GDPR↔CRA)**: CRA user opt-out vs. DORA mandatory monitoring. Scope-disjoint test: **Conditional (Y when the DORA entity deploys CRA-regulated products)**. No reclassification required on the relationship itself (still COMPLEMENTARY), but the conflict remains flagged.
- **CRA ↔ AI_Act** — COMPLEMENTARY (SAME): CRA product-level on-device internal-activity monitoring + AI Act post-market monitoring stack for AI-enabled products. Trigger alignment DIFFERENT but co-applicable (CRA access/modification of data/services/functions vs. AI Act high-risk AI system post-market data); threshold tension NONE (CRA support-period vs. AI Act qualitative proportionality). The AI Act layer adds the feedback-loop dimension (analysis-to-risk-management loop per Art. 72(1)–(2) `actively and systematically collect, document and analyse`). Scope-disjoint test: **Conditional (Y when the CRA-regulated product is also a high-risk AI system)**. No reclassification required.
- **DORA ↔ AI_Act** — COMPLEMENTARY (SAME): DORA entity-level monitoring (Art. 8(1) continuous monitoring) + AI Act post-market monitoring (Art. 72(1)–(2) lifecycle monitoring) stack for DORA financial entities deploying high-risk AI. Trigger alignment PARTIAL; threshold tension NONE (DORA continuous vs. AI Act qualitative proportionality); recipient DORA NCA vs. AI Act MSA + AI Office. Scope-disjoint test: **Y (when a DORA financial entity deploys high-risk AI)**. No reclassification required.

The continuous-monitoring architecture is **legitimately diverse with one CRDA-flagged genuine tension to resolve at the OJ level** — each regulator imposes a distinct monitoring mandate under a distinct anchor at a distinct scope layer: GDPR (entity-level 5 Art. 32(2) risk types with breach-`becoming-aware` clock for Art. 33(1) notification); NIS 2 (entity-level all-hazards detection capability as incident-handling dimension per Art. 21(2)(b) — partial coverage); CRA (product-level on-device internal-activity monitoring with **user opt-out** + vulnerability reporting per Annex I Part I (2)(l) + Annex I Part II (6)); DORA (entity-level continuous monitoring of ICT-system security and functioning per Art. 8(1) OJ-literal locus Art. 9(1) + prompt anomalous-activity detection per Art. 10(1)+(2)); AI Act (AI-system-level post-market monitoring per Art. 72(1)–(2) qualitative proportionality). The structural CRA user opt-out vs. mandatory-monitoring conflict propagates to GDPR↔CRA, NIS2↔CRA, CRA↔DORA — three pairs carry the same GENUINE TENSION flag.

#### Cross-recipient map

D-10.1 generates a layered recipient set on the **continuous-monitoring evidence trail**. The recipients are at distinct regulatory channels on the same monitoring-data object but the channels do not generate a notification cascade of D-10.1 itself — they are **monitoring-evidence channels** that feed distinct regulatory artefacts:

- **GDPR** — data subject + supervisory authority (DPA) on the Art. 32(2) monitoring evidence + Art. 33(1) breach-`becoming-aware` clock.
- **NIS 2** — management body + competent authority (CSIRT or NCA) on the Art. 21(2)(b) incident-handling detection capability (partial-coverage dimension).
- **CRA** — market surveillance authority (MSA) + users on the Annex I Part I (2)(l) on-device monitoring with user opt-out + Annex I Part II (6) vulnerability-reporting contact address.
- **DORA** — national competent authority (NCA) on the Art. 8(1) continuous monitoring (OJ-literal locus Art. 9(1)) + Art. 10(1)+(2) prompt anomalous-activity detection.
- **AI Act** — market surveillance authority (MSA) + AI Office + provider on the Art. 72(1)–(2) post-market monitoring system + Art. 74 market-surveillance cooperation.

These are distinct regulatory channels on the monitoring-data object. **The cross-recipient map does NOT generate a notification cascade of D-10.1 itself** — the continuous-monitoring duty is upstream of any breach notification (cross to D-04.3 Regulatory Notification, D-04.4 Data Restoration & Recovery). The D-10.1 monitoring evidence feeds the D-04 incident-response chain (D-04.1 Detection & Triage, D-04.2 Containment & Mitigation, D-04.3 Regulatory Notification, D-04.4 Data Restoration & Recovery) and the D-09.4 Records of Processing documentation obligation. **The CRDA-flagged CRA-opt-out vs. mandatory-monitoring tension has no recipient — the conflict is between the regimes' substance, not their recipients.**

#### D-10.1.0 — High-level SecurityObjective

**Objective.** Continuous security monitoring is established through a **layered detection-and-monitoring architecture** spanning personal-data processing systems, network and information systems, on-device product activity, ICT systems and tools, and high-risk AI systems, with detection capability feeding the incident-response chain.

**Considerations.**
- 5 of 5 regulations participate with **distinct anchors and scope layers** — GDPR (Art. 32(2) 5-risk-type detection + Art. 33(1) 72-hour breach-`becoming-aware` clock — entity-level personal-data monitoring); NIS 2 (Art. 21(2)(b) all-hazards detection capability as incident-handling dimension — **partial coverage, no dedicated continuous-monitoring mandate**); CRA (Annex I Part I (2)(l) on-device internal-activity monitoring with **user opt-out** + Annex I Part II (6) vulnerability reporting — product-level); DORA (Art. 8(1) `continuously monitor and control the security and functioning of ICT systems and tools` (OJ-literal locus Art. 9(1) per SR-DORA-030) + Art. 10(1)+(2) prompt anomalous-activity detection + Art. 7(2) continuous ICT risk identification — financial-entity); AI Act (Art. 72(1)-(2) `actively and systematically collect, document and analyse` post-market monitoring `proportionate to the nature of the AI technologies and the risks` + Art. 74 market-surveillance cooperation — AI-system lifecycle).
- **CRDA-deep verified (10 pairs: 9 SAME — COMPLEMENTARY + 1 SAME — EQUAL NIS 2 ↔ DORA on continuous-monitoring dimension, DORA-dominant for financial entities)** — no CORRECTED reclassifications, no scope-disjoint pairs.
- **1 CRDA-flagged GENUINE TENSION — Layer 2 OJ-level resolution required**: CRA Annex I Part I (2)(l) user opt-out vs. mandatory-monitoring regimes (GDPR Art. 32(2) + NIS 2 Art. 21(2)(b) + DORA Art. 8(1)). The conflict propagates to 3 of 10 pairs (GDPR↔CRA primary, NIS2↔CRA, CRA↔DORA). The OJ does not resolve the conflict. The two regimes can stack on the layered monitoring architecture (entity-layer mandatory + product-layer with opt-out), but the user's opt-out from CRA monitoring may not legally exempt the controller/NSIE/DORA entity from the respective mandatory-monitoring regime. Phase 1C Doc 07 must perform the Layer 2 OJ-level resolution.
- **Scope caveats**: (a) NIS 2 ↔ DORA EQUAL discharges both on the DORA-dominant stack for financial entities; (b) DORA-led stack for financial entities — DORA Art. 8(1) monitoring is broader (security AND functioning) than GDPR Art. 32(2) 5-risk-type, with GDPR detection as a sub-component; (c) AI Act post-market monitoring adds the analysis-to-risk-management feedback loop (Art. 72(2) → Art. 9 + Art. 17) not present in the other 4 regulators.

#### Sub-domain scope structure (verified by CRDA-deep)

The 5 participating regulations each define D-10.1 over a distinct **monitoring mandate** of the continuous-monitoring architecture; the anchor, scope dimension, trigger, threshold, recipient, and content template all depend on which perspective the regulation takes on the monitoring object.

| Axis (reg) | Monitoring mandate | Anchor (OJ-literal) | Scope layer | Recipient | Legal anchor |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| **GDPR** | entity-level 5-risk-type detection (accidental or unlawful destruction, loss, alteration, unauthorised disclosure of, or access to personal data) + breach-`becoming-aware` clock for Art. 33(1) 72-hour notification | Art. 32(2) chapeau `the ability to ensure the ongoing confidentiality, integrity, availability and resilience of processing systems and services` + Art. 32(1)(d) `a process for regularly testing, assessing and evaluating` + Art. 33(1) `without undue delay and, where feasible, not later than 72 hours after having become aware of it` | entity (controller / processor) on personal-data processing systems | data subject + DPA — Art. 32(2) detection evidence + Art. 33(1) notification | Art. 32(1)(b) + Art. 32(1)(d) + Art. 32(2)(b)(c)(d) + Art. 33(1) `becoming aware` |
| **NIS2** | all-hazards detection capability as incident-handling dimension (partial coverage — no dedicated continuous-monitoring mandate) | Art. 21(2)(b) `incident handling` detection capability | entity (essential / important entity) on network and information systems | management body + competent authority (CSIRT) — Art. 21(2)(b) detection capability + incident-handling 6-action lifecycle per SR-NIS2-008 (prevent, detect, analyse, contain, respond, recover) | Art. 21(2)(b) + Art. 21(1) chapeau all-hazards + Art. 21(9) significant-incident notification chain (cross to D-04.3) |
| **CRA** | product-level on-device internal-activity monitoring with user opt-out + vulnerability-reporting mechanism | Annex I Part I (2)(l) `provide security-related information by recording and monitoring relevant internal activity, including access to or modification of data, services or functions, and provide an opt-out mechanism for the user` + Annex I Part II (6) `facilitate the sharing of information about potential vulnerabilities of the product with digital elements` | product (product with digital elements placed on the market) | MSA + users — Annex I Part I (2)(l) on-device monitoring + Annex I Part II (6) vulnerability-reporting contact address | Annex I Part I (2)(l) + Annex I Part II (6) + Art. 13(8) manufacturer policies/processes/procedures (cross to D-09.1) + Annex VII §2(b) CVD policy (cross to D-02.3) + Annex VII §6 test reports (cross to D-10.3) |
| **DORA** | entity-level continuous monitoring of ICT-system security and functioning + prompt anomalous-activity detection + continuous ICT risk identification | Art. 8(1) `Financial entities shall continuously monitor and control the security and functioning of ICT systems and tools and shall minimise the impact of ICT risk on ICT systems through the deployment of appropriate ICT security tools, policies and procedures` per `SR-DORA-030` (OJ-literal locus Art. 9(1)) + Art. 10(1) `promptly detect anomalous activities` + Art. 7(2) continuous ICT risk identification | entity (financial entity under DORA scope per Art. 2) | NCA + management body — Art. 8(1) continuous monitoring + Art. 10(1)+(2) multi-layered controls + EBA Guidelines supervisory baseline | Art. 8(1) per `SR-DORA-030` (OJ-literal locus Art. 9(1)) + Art. 10(1) + Art. 10(2) multiple layers + alert thresholds + auto-alert + Art. 7(2) continuous basis + yearly review + Art. 4 proportionality |
| **AI_Act** | AI-system-level post-market monitoring system + market-surveillance cooperation | Art. 72(1) `Providers shall establish and document a post-market monitoring system in a manner that is proportionate to the nature of the AI technologies and the risks of the high-risk AI system` + Art. 72(2) `The post-market monitoring system shall actively and systematically collect, document and analyse relevant data` + Art. 74(1) market-surveillance cooperation under Regulation (EU) 2019/1020 | AI-system (high-risk AI system per Art. 6) | MSA + AI Office + provider — Art. 72(1)–(2) post-market monitoring system + Art. 74(1) market-surveillance cooperation | Art. 72(1) + Art. 72(2) `actively and systematically collect, document and analyse` 3-way verb AND + Art. 72(3) post-market monitoring plan + Art. 74(1) market-surveillance cooperation + Art. 6 high-risk classification + Art. 9 risk-management-system documentation (cross to D-09.1) |

#### Scope-overlap matrix (5×5)

The scope-overlap column in the matrix below indicates whether the same event can trigger both regulations on a single party; this is the gating condition for the high-level SO to engage.

| | GDPR | NIS2 | CRA | DORA | AI_Act |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| **GDPR** | — | Y (controller = NSIE; detection capability overlaps — risk-type-triggered vs. incident-triggered) | **Y (CRDA-flagged tension)** (controller-manufacturer; CRA opt-out vs. GDPR mandatory — conflict requires Layer 2 OJ-level resolution) | Y (DORA financial entity = controller; DORA-led stack with GDPR 5-risk-type as sub-component) | Conditional (Y when high-risk AI processes personal data — provider is controller) |
| **NIS2** | Y (same; reciprocal — controller-NSIE) | — | **Conditional (CRDA-flagged tension propagated)** (Y when NSIE deploys CRA-regulated products; CRA opt-out vs. NIS 2 mandatory monitoring — same flag) | Y (DORA financial entity = NSIE; DORA-dominant continuous monitoring discharges both — EQUAL) | Conditional (Y when NSIE deploys high-risk AI) |
| **CRA** | **Y (CRDA-flagged tension)** (same; reciprocal — controller-manufacturer; CRA opt-out vs. GDPR mandatory) | **Conditional (CRDA-flagged tension propagated)** (same; reciprocal — NSIE deploys CRA-regulated; CRA opt-out vs. NIS 2 mandatory) | — | **Conditional (CRDA-flagged tension propagated)** (Y when DORA entity deploys CRA-regulated; CRA opt-out vs. DORA mandatory) | Conditional (Y when CRA-regulated product is also high-risk AI; CRA Annex I conformity + AI Act Art. 72 stack — no OJ-level consolidation) |
| **DORA** | Y (same; reciprocal — DORA financial entity = controller; DORA-led stack) | Y (same; reciprocal — DORA financial entity = NSIE; DORA-dominant — EQUAL) | **Conditional (CRDA-flagged tension propagated)** (same; reciprocal — DORA entity deploys CRA-regulated; CRA opt-out vs. DORA mandatory) | — | Y (DORA financial entity deploys high-risk AI) |
| **AI_Act** | Conditional (same; reciprocal — provider/deployer = controller) | Conditional (same; reciprocal — NSIE deploys high-risk AI) | Conditional (same; reciprocal — CRA-regulated product is high-risk AI) | Y (same; reciprocal — DORA financial entity deploys high-risk AI) | — |

Legend: **Y** = scope overlap on a single event at a single party (high-level SO engages on the joint-condition operator). **Conditional (Y/N)** = conditional overlap depending on entity-product combination. **No N (typically) pairs** — D-10.1 has no scope-disjoint structure. **CRDA-flagged tension** = the CRA user opt-out (Annex I Part I (2)(l)) conflicts with the mandatory-monitoring regime (GDPR Art. 32(2), NIS 2 Art. 21(2)(b), or DORA Art. 8(1)); the OJ does not resolve the conflict; Layer 2 review required. CRDA-deep scope-disjoint tests: GDPR↔NIS2 = Y; GDPR↔CRA = Y (CRDA-flagged tension); GDPR↔DORA = Y; GDPR↔AI_Act = Conditional (Y when high-risk AI processes personal data); NIS2↔CRA = Conditional (Y when NSIE deploys CRA-regulated — CRDA-flagged tension); NIS2↔DORA = Y; NIS2↔AI_Act = Conditional (Y when NSIE deploys high-risk AI); CRA↔DORA = Conditional (Y when DORA entity deploys CRA-regulated — CRDA-flagged tension); CRA↔AI_Act = Conditional (Y when CRA-regulated product is high-risk AI); DORA↔AI_Act = Y.

#### Scope-overlap principle (operational)

The high-level SO (`SO-D-10.1.HL`) engages on a given party **if and only if ≥2 applicable regulations have ≥1 Y or Conditional-Y-scope-overlap pair on the same party** (e.g. a DORA financial entity that is also a controller processing personal data through high-risk AI — a vertically-integrated financial-controller-AI-provider with GDPR + DORA + AI Act applicability on the layered 5-monitoring-architecture). For a CRA-regulated manufacturer that is also a controller (controller-manufacturer), the GDPR + CRA sub-SOs activate on **two different artefacts** (entity-level 5-risk-type monitoring vs. product-level on-device internal-activity monitoring) — the two regimes meet at the controller-manufacturer interface, **but the CRDA-flagged GENUINE TENSION (CRA user opt-out vs. GDPR mandatory monitoring) requires Layer 2 OJ-level resolution**. For an entity with GDPR + CRA + DORA applicability, the three sub-SOs activate with the CRA opt-out conflict propagating against both GDPR and DORA mandatory monitoring. The five monitoring mandates are stacked, not collapsed: 5-risk-type detection (GDPR) + all-hazards incident-handling detection capability (NIS 2 partial) + on-device internal-activity monitoring with user opt-out (CRA) + continuous ICT-system monitoring (DORA) + AI-system post-market monitoring (AI Act). The high-level SO surfaces the common continuous-monitoring duty but does **NOT** collapse the per-reg anchor, scope dimension, trigger, threshold, recipient, or content template, **and does NOT resolve the CRDA-flagged GENUINE TENSION**.

#### Aggregation logic (from CRDA-deep verified classifications)

- **5 of 5 regulations participate** (GDPR, NIS 2, CRA, DORA, AI Act — all five participate on the monitoring object): GDPR (Art. 32(2) 5-risk-type detection + Art. 33(1) breach-`becoming-aware` clock — entity-level personal-data monitoring); NIS 2 (Art. 21(2)(b) all-hazards detection capability as incident-handling dimension — partial coverage); CRA (Annex I Part I (2)(l) on-device internal-activity monitoring with user opt-out + Annex I Part II (6) vulnerability reporting — product-level); DORA (Art. 8(1) per `SR-DORA-030` OJ-literal locus Art. 9(1) continuous monitoring + Art. 10(1)+(2) prompt anomalous-activity detection + Art. 7(2) continuous ICT risk identification — financial-entity continuous monitoring); AI Act (Art. 72(1)–(2) post-market monitoring `proportionate to the nature of the AI technologies and the risks` + Art. 74 market-surveillance cooperation — AI-system lifecycle monitoring).
- **10 pairwise cells, verified by CRDA-deep**:
  GDPR↔NIS2 (SAME — COMPLEMENTARY — risk-type-triggered vs. incident-triggered; partial trigger alignment);
  GDPR↔CRA (SAME — COMPLEMENTARY — **CRDA-flagged GENUINE TENSION** on CRA user opt-out vs. GDPR mandatory monitoring — Layer 2 OJ-level resolution required; OJ does not resolve);
  GDPR↔DORA (SAME — COMPLEMENTARY — DORA broader (security AND functioning) than GDPR (5 risk types); DORA subsumes GDPR detection capability within entity-level continuous-monitoring for financial entities);
  GDPR↔AI_Act (SAME — COMPLEMENTARY — pre-breach detection vs. lifecycle; different trigger alignment);
  NIS2↔CRA (SAME — COMPLEMENTARY — **CRDA-flagged GENUINE TENSION propagated** — CRA user opt-out vs. NIS 2 mandatory monitoring);
  NIS2↔DORA (SAME — EQUAL — both regulations impose continuous-monitoring obligations; DORA-dominant for financial entities under DORA's direct scope);
  NIS2↔AI_Act (SAME — COMPLEMENTARY — entity-level operational vs. AI-system lifecycle; different trigger alignment);
  CRA↔DORA (SAME — COMPLEMENTARY — **CRDA-flagged GENUINE TENSION propagated** — CRA user opt-out vs. DORA mandatory monitoring);
  CRA↔AI_Act (SAME — COMPLEMENTARY — CRA Annex I conformity + AI Act post-market monitoring stack for AI-enabled products; AI Act adds feedback-loop dimension);
  DORA↔AI_Act (SAME — COMPLEMENTARY — DORA continuous monitoring + AI Act post-market monitoring stack for DORA financial entities deploying high-risk AI).
- The five participating regulations impose **distinct monitoring mandates** at **distinct scope layers** with **distinct anchors** and **distinct content templates**: GDPR (entity-level Art. 32(2) 5-risk-type detection with breach-`becoming-aware` clock — not codified for monitoring content; entity-discretionary under Art. 24(1) chapeau); NIS 2 (entity-level Art. 21(2)(b) incident-handling detection capability as partial coverage; no dedicated continuous-monitoring mandate); CRA (product-level Annex I Part I (2)(l) on-device internal-activity monitoring with user opt-out — partially codified for `recording and monitoring relevant internal activity, including access to or modification of data, services or functions`; Annex VII §6 test reports + Annex VII §2(b) CVD policy for evidence); DORA (entity-level Art. 8(1) per `SR-DORA-030` OJ-literal locus Art. 9(1) continuous monitoring + Art. 10(1)+(2) multi-layered detection — content not codified; entity-discretionary under Art. 4 proportionality); AI Act (AI-system Art. 72(1)–(2) post-market monitoring with `proportionate to the nature of the AI technologies and the risks` qualitative proportionality — content partially codified for `actively and systematically collect, document and analyse`; Art. 74 market-surveillance cooperation borrows Regulation (EU) 2019/1020 framework). The thresholds align qualitatively except for GDPR's 72-hour breach-`becoming-aware` clock (quantitative; cross to D-04.3 Regulatory Notification) and CRA's support-period obligation (cross to D-09.4 Records of Processing + D-06.3 Contractual Security Obligations).
- The high-level SO describes the **COMMON OBJECTIVE** (continuous or quasi-continuous monitoring of ICT systems + internal activity + AI-system post-market data with detection capability and pre-breach-notification readiness + cross-recipient evidence trail); the **per-regulation perspective** (mandate, scope layer, anchor, content template, threshold, recipient, evidence template) is preserved verbatim in the five sub-SOs.
- The high-level SO does **NOT** resolve the CRDA-flagged GENUINE TENSION — **1 conditional emergent tension on CRA user opt-out vs. mandatory-monitoring regimes (GDPR Art. 32(2), NIS 2 Art. 21(2)(b), DORA Art. 8(1))**, propagating to 3 of 10 pairs (GDPR↔CRA primary, NIS2↔CRA, CRA↔DORA). Phase 1C Doc 07 must perform the **Layer 2 OJ-level resolution** because no OJ-level consolidation rule exists for the CRA opt-out vs. mandatory-monitoring conflict. The remaining 9 pairs are SAME — COMPLEMENTARY (8) + SAME — EQUAL (1) with no scope-disjoint structure.
- **Scope caveat (CRITICAL — CRDA-flagged GENUINE TENSION on CRA user opt-out vs. mandatory monitoring)** — derived from CRDA-deep verified classifications: CRA Annex I Part I (2)(l) allows user opt-out from on-device internal-activity monitoring; GDPR Art. 32(2) entity-level 5-risk-type monitoring is mandatory; NIS 2 Art. 21(2)(b) all-hazards detection capability is mandatory; DORA Art. 8(1) continuous monitoring is mandatory. For a CRA-regulated product processing personal data, the CRA user opt-out conflicts with the GDPR mandatory monitoring (primary tension on the GDPR↔CRA pair); the same structural conflict propagates to NIS 2 ↔ CRA (when the NSIE deploys CRA-regulated products) and CRA ↔ DORA (when the DORA entity deploys CRA-regulated products). The OJ does not resolve this conflict. Phase 1 entities with this conflict must (a) maintain the mandatory monitoring under GDPR / NIS 2 / DORA on the entity layer regardless of the CRA opt-out at the product layer, and (b) escalate to Layer 2 OJ-level resolution for the cross-layer conflict. The two regimes can stack on the layered monitoring architecture (entity-layer mandatory + product-layer with opt-out), but the user's opt-out from CRA monitoring may not legally exempt the controller from GDPR Art. 32(2) mandatory detection.
- **Scope caveat (CRITICAL — NIS 2 ↔ DORA EQUAL classification on continuous-monitoring dimension)** — derived from CRDA-deep verified classifications: both regulations impose continuous-monitoring obligations (NIS 2 Art. 21(2)(b) detection capability vs. DORA Art. 8(1) per `SR-DORA-030` OJ-literal locus Art. 9(1) `continuously monitor and control the security and functioning of ICT systems and tools`). For DORA financial entities, the DORA Art. 9(1) continuous monitoring discharges both DORA's dedicated monitoring obligation and NIS 2's Art. 21(2)(b) partial-coverage detection dimension for DORA financial entities that are also NSIEs. For non-financial NSIEs, NIS 2 is the authority. The EQUAL classification preserves the OJ literal parallel between the two regimes on the continuous-monitoring dimension; the dominant-authority reading is the operational consequence of the DORA-direct-applicability vs NIS 2-MS-transposition asymmetry.
- **Scope caveat (CRITICAL — DORA-led stack for financial entities)** — derived from CRDA-deep verified classifications: DORA Art. 8(1) continuous monitoring is broader (security AND functioning) than GDPR Art. 32(2) 5-risk-type detection. For DORA financial entities that are also controllers, the DORA Art. 8(1) / Art. 9(1) continuous monitoring discharges the financial entity's continuous-monitoring obligation and the GDPR detection capability becomes a sub-component within the DORA-led stack. The DORA Art. 10(1) prompt anomalous-activity detection + multi-layered controls + alert thresholds + auto-alert to relevant staff add the operational detection layer to the continuous-monitoring mandate.
- **Scope caveat (CRITICAL — AI Act post-market monitoring feedback loop)** — derived from CRDA-deep verified classifications: AI Act Art. 72(2) `actively and systematically collect, document and analyse relevant data` adds the feedback-loop dimension (analysis-to-risk-management loop) that none of the other 4 regulators impose in equivalent form. The AI Act post-market monitoring data feeds back into the AI-Act provider's risk-management-system documentation (Art. 9 — cross to D-09.1) and the AI-system quality management system (Art. 17 — cross to D-09.1). The analysis-to-risk-management loop is AI-Act-specific and is preserved in the AI Act sub-SO.

#### D-10.1.1 — Sub-SO for GDPR

```yaml
id: SO-D-10.1.GDPR
subdomain: D-10.1
applies_to: [GDPR]
inherits_from: SO-GDPR-030 + SO-GDPR-008
source_SR: SR-GDPR-019 + SR-GDPR-020 + SR-GDPR-021
activation: if GDPR in applicable_regs
phase_1A_role: 1_of_5
verified_relationship: COMPLEMENTARY (CRDA-deep, D-10.1: GDPR↔NIS2 = COMPLEMENTARY (SAME — risk-type-triggered vs. incident-triggered; partial trigger alignment); GDPR↔CRA = COMPLEMENTARY (SAME — **CRDA-flagged GENUINE TENSION** on CRA user opt-out vs. GDPR mandatory monitoring — Layer 2 OJ-level resolution required); GDPR↔DORA = COMPLEMENTARY (SAME — DORA broader than GDPR; DORA-led stack with GDPR detection as sub-component); GDPR↔AI_Act = COMPLEMENTARY (SAME — pre-breach detection vs. lifecycle; different trigger alignment))
```

**Objective.** The controller and processor implement appropriate technical and organisational measures to ensure the **ongoing confidentiality, integrity, availability and resilience of processing systems and services** (Art. 32(1)(b) GDPR) and **regularly test, assess and evaluate their effectiveness** (Art. 32(1)(d) GDPR); personal-data processing systems and services are monitored for the five Art. 32(2) risk types, with a clear definition of the moment of `becoming aware of` a breach to anchor the Art. 33(1) 72-hour notification clock.

**CSF anchors (from `SR-GDPR-019` + `SR-GDPR-020` v0.3 security_rationale):**
DE.CM-01 (primary), DE.CM-09, DE.AE-02, ID.IM-04.
*(DE.CM-01 — Networks and network services are monitored to find potentially adverse events — anchors the GDPR Art. 32(2) 5-risk-type monitoring mapped to NIST CSF 2.0 DE.CM-01; DE.CM-09 — Computing hardware and software, runtime environments, and their data are monitored — anchors the personal-data processing systems monitoring per DomainAnalysis cross-validation; DE.AE-02 — Adverse events are analyzed to identify or determine the scope of an incident — anchors the breach-`becoming-aware` clock per Art. 33(1); ID.IM-04 — Adequate resource capacity to ensure availability is maintained — anchors the Art. 32(1)(b) ongoing availability dimension. The four subcategories together enable the controller or processor to demonstrate ex post, against the Art. 5(2) accountability burden, that the 5-risk-type monitoring was operational and that the breach-`becoming-aware` clock was correctly anchored for Art. 33(1) notification.)*

#### D-10.1.2 — Sub-SO for NIS 2

```yaml
id: SO-D-10.1.NIS2
subdomain: D-10.1

source_SR: SR-NIS2-009 + SR-NIS2-014
activation: if NIS2 in applicable_regs
phase_1A_role: 2_of_5
verified_relationship: COMPLEMENTARY (CRDA-deep, D-10.1: GDPR↔NIS2 = COMPLEMENTARY (SAME — risk-type-triggered vs. incident-triggered); NIS2↔CRA = COMPLEMENTARY (SAME — **CRDA-flagged GENUINE TENSION propagated** — CRA user opt-out vs. NIS 2 mandatory monitoring); NIS2↔DORA = EQUAL (SAME — both impose continuous-monitoring obligations; DORA-dominant for financial entities under DORA's direct scope); NIS2↔AI_Act = COMPLEMENTARY (SAME — entity-level operational vs. AI-system lifecycle; different trigger alignment))
```

**Objective.** Essential and important entities establish an **incident-handling capability** covering prevention, detection, analysis, containment, response, and recovery of incidents (Art. 21(2)(b) NIS 2); the detection dimension is the partial-coverage dimension of D-10.1, operating on an all-hazards basis and feeding the Art. 23(1) significant-incident notification chain.

**CSF anchors (from `SR-NIS2-009` + `SR-NIS2-014` v0.3 security_rationale):**
DE.CM-01 (primary), DE.CM-09, DE.AE-02, ID.IM-04.
*(DE.CM-01 — Networks and network services are monitored to find potentially adverse events — anchors the NIS 2 Art. 21(2)(b) incident-handling detection capability mapped to NIST CSF 2.0 DE.CM-01; DE.CM-09 — Computing hardware and software, runtime environments, and their data are monitored — anchors the all-hazards network and information systems monitoring per DomainAnalysis cross-validation; DE.AE-02 — Adverse events are analyzed to identify or determine the scope of an incident — anchors the Art. 23 significant-incident detection trigger; ID.IM-04 — Adequate resource capacity — anchors the prevention+detection+response feedback loop. The four subcategories together enable the NSIE to demonstrate ex post, against the competent authority (CSIRT), that the Art. 21(2)(b) detection capability was operational on the all-hazards basis and that the Art. 23(1) significant-incident notification was correctly triggered. For DORA financial entities, the DORA Art. 9(1) continuous monitoring discharges both obligations — DORA-dominant.)*


**Objective.** A product with digital elements **provides security-related information by recording and monitoring relevant internal activity, including access to or modification of data, services or functions, and provides an opt-out mechanism for the user** (Annex I Part I (2)(l) CRA); the manufacturer **facilitates the sharing of information about potential vulnerabilities** through a single contact address (Annex I Part II (6) CRA).

**CSF anchors (from `SR-CRA-100` + `SR-CRA-101` v0.3 security_rationale):**
DE.CM-01 (primary), DE.CM-09, ID.IM-04.
*(DE.CM-01 — Networks and network services are monitored to find potentially adverse events — anchors the CRA Annex I Part I (2)(l) on-device internal-activity monitoring mapped to NIST CSF 2.0 DE.CM-01; DE.CM-09 — Computing hardware and software, runtime environments, and their data are monitored — anchors the product-level on-device monitoring per DomainAnalysis cross-validation; ID.IM-04 — Adequate resource capacity — anchors the manufacturer-side observability dimension. The three subcategories together enable the manufacturer to demonstrate ex post, against the MSA, that the Annex I Part I (2)(l) on-device monitoring was operational with the user opt-out mechanism in place, and that the Annex I Part II (6) vulnerability-reporting contact address was functional. **IMPORTANT — CRA opt-out vs. mandatory-monitoring conflict**: per CRDA-deep §D-10.1 lines 29, 94, 106, 217, 340, the CRA Annex I Part I (2)(l) user opt-out conflicts with GDPR Art. 32(2) mandatory monitoring, NIS 2 Art. 21(2)(b) mandatory detection capability, and DORA Art. 8(1) mandatory continuous monitoring. The OJ does not resolve this conflict. Phase 1 entities with this conflict must (a) maintain the mandatory monitoring under GDPR / NIS 2 / DORA on the entity layer regardless of the CRA opt-out at the product layer, and (b) escalate to Layer 2 OJ-level resolution for the cross-layer conflict.)*

#### D-10.1.4 — Sub-SO for DORA

```yaml
id: SO-D-10.1.DORA

activation: if DORA in applicable_regs
phase_1A_role: 4_of_5

```

**Objective.** Financial entities **continuously monitor and control the security and functioning of ICT systems and tools** and **minimise the impact of ICT risk on ICT systems** (Art. 8(1) DORA per SR-DORA-030; OJ-literal locus Art. 9(1)); **anomalous activities are promptly detected** through multi-layered controls, alert thresholds, incident response triggers, and identification of single points of failure (Art. 10(1)+(2) DORA), with sources of ICT risk **identified on a continuous basis** and risk scenarios reviewed on a regular basis, at least yearly (Art. 7(2) DORA).

**CSF anchors (from `SR-DORA-030` + `SR-DORA-011` + `SR-DORA-012` + `SR-DORA-004` v0.3 security_rationale):**
DE.CM-01 (primary), DE.CM-09, PR.PS-04, DE.AE-02, ID.RA-01, ID.RA-03.
*(DE.CM-01 — Networks and network services are monitored to find potentially adverse events — anchors the DORA Art. 8(1) `continuously monitor and control` mapped to NIST CSF 2.0 DE.CM-01; DE.CM-09 — Computing hardware and software, runtime environments, and their data are monitored — anchors the financial-entity ICT-systems monitoring per DomainAnalysis cross-validation; PR.PS-04 — Software installation, operation, and updates are performed consistent with the security policy — anchors the Art. 8(1) `tools, policies and procedures` 3-way AND; DE.AE-02 — Adverse events are analyzed to identify or determine the scope of an incident — anchors the Art. 10(1) prompt anomalous-activity detection; ID.RA-01 — Asset vulnerabilities are identified, validated, and recorded — anchors the Art. 7(2) continuous ICT risk identification + yearly review; ID.RA-03 — Threats to assets are identified and recorded — anchors the Art. 7(2) `cyber threats and ICT vulnerabilities`. The six subcategories together enable the financial entity to demonstrate ex post, against the NCA + EBA Guidelines supervisory baseline, that the Art. 8(1) continuous monitoring + Art. 10(1)+(2) multi-layered detection + Art. 7(2) continuous ICT risk identification were operational in light of the proportionality factors (size, nature, scale, complexity per Art. 4). For DORA financial entities that are also NIS 2 NSIEs, the DORA Art. 9(1) continuous monitoring discharges both obligations — DORA-dominant. For DORA financial entities that are also controllers, the GDPR Art. 32(2) 5-risk-type monitoring becomes a sub-component within the DORA-led stack. **CRDA-flagged GENUINE TENSION with CRA (propagated)**: CRA Annex I Part I (2)(l) user opt-out vs. DORA Art. 8(1) mandatory continuous monitoring — Layer 2 OJ-level resolution required.)*

The three subcategories together enable the AI Act provider to demonstrate ex post, against the MSA + AI Office supervisory baseline, that the Art. 72(1)–(2) post-market monitoring system was operational with the `proportionate to the nature of the AI technologies and the risks` proportionality calibration, and that the `actively and systematically collect, document and analyse` 3-way verb AND was operational across the AI-system lifecycle. The Art. 74(1) market-surveillance cooperation under Regulation (EU) 2019/1020 provides the MSA + AI Office recipient channel.)*

---

#### Emergent tensions (from CRDA-deep §D-10.1)

**1 conditional emergent tension** (CRDA-flagged GENUINE TENSION — Layer 2 OJ-level resolution required):

**Tension #1: CRA Annex I Part I (2)(l) user opt-out vs. mandatory-monitoring regimes (GDPR Art. 32(2), NIS 2 Art. 21(2)(b), DORA Art. 8(1)) — CRDA-flagged, OJ does not resolve.**

The CRA Annex I Part I (2)(l) `provide security-related information by recording and monitoring relevant internal activity, including access to or modification of data, services or functions, and provide an opt-out mechanism for the user` clause creates a structural conflict with the three mandatory-monitoring regimes:

- **GDPR ↔ CRA** (primary tension, CRDA line 106): CRA user opt-out allows the user to disable on-device internal-activity monitoring; GDPR Art. 32(2) entity-level 5-risk-type monitoring is mandatory for the controller. For a CRA-regulated product processing personal data, the user's opt-out from CRA product-level monitoring does not legally exempt the controller from GDPR Art. 32(2) entity-level mandatory detection. The OJ does not resolve this conflict.
- **NIS 2 ↔ CRA** (propagated tension, CRDA line 217): same structural conflict — CRA user opt-out vs. NIS 2 Art. 21(2)(b) entity-level all-hazards detection capability (mandatory). For an NSIE deploying CRA-regulated products, the user's opt-out from CRA product-level monitoring does not legally exempt the NSIE from NIS 2 mandatory detection.
- **CRA ↔ DORA** (propagated tension, CRDA line 340): same structural conflict — CRA user opt-out vs. DORA Art. 8(1) entity-level continuous monitoring (mandatory). For a DORA entity deploying CRA-regulated products, the user's opt-out from CRA product-level monitoring does not legally exempt the DORA entity from DORA Art. 8(1) mandatory continuous monitoring.

**Operational consequence.** The two regimes can stack on the layered monitoring architecture (entity-layer mandatory + product-layer with opt-out) — the controller / NSIE / DORA entity must maintain the mandatory monitoring on the entity layer regardless of the CRA opt-out at the product layer. The user's opt-out from CRA monitoring does not legally exempt the controller / NSIE / DORA entity from the respective mandatory-monitoring regime. **Phase 1C Doc 07 must perform the Layer 2 OJ-level resolution** because no OJ-level consolidation rule exists for the CRA opt-out vs. mandatory-monitoring conflict — the OJ explicitly permits the CRA opt-out without resolving how the opt-out interacts with the GDPR / NIS 2 / DORA mandatory monitoring.

**No scope-disjoint pairs** at D-10.1 — all 10 pairs pass the scope-disjoint test (Y or Conditional-Y-on-same-party). **No CONTRADICTORY pairs** on the monitoring-duty itself. **No CORRECTED reclassifications.** The remaining 7 pairs (GDPR↔NIS2, GDPR↔DORA, GDPR↔AI_Act, NIS2↔AI_Act, CRA↔AI_Act, DORA↔AI_Act) are SAME — COMPLEMENTARY without emergent tension. The **NIS 2 ↔ DORA** pair is SAME — EQUAL (both regulations impose continuous-monitoring obligations; DORA-dominant for financial entities) without emergent tension.

The **NIS 2 ↔ DORA EQUAL classification on continuous-monitoring dimension** is the structurally significant EQUAL pair: both regulations impose continuous-monitoring obligations (NIS 2 Art. 21(2)(b) detection capability vs. DORA Art. 8(1) per `SR-DORA-030` OJ-literal locus Art. 9(1) `continuously monitor and control the security and functioning of ICT systems and tools`). For DORA financial entities, the DORA Art. 9(1) continuous monitoring discharges both DORA's dedicated monitoring obligation and NIS 2's Art. 21(2)(b) partial-coverage detection dimension. For non-financial NSIEs, NIS 2 is the authority. The EQUAL classification preserves the OJ literal parallel between the two regimes on the continuous-monitoring dimension; the dominant-authority reading is the operational consequence of the DORA-direct-applicability vs NIS 2-MS-transposition asymmetry.

The **DORA-led stack for financial entities** is the structurally significant COMPLEMENTARY pair: DORA Art. 8(1) continuous monitoring is broader (security AND functioning) than GDPR Art. 32(2) 5-risk-type detection. For DORA financial entities that are also controllers, the DORA Art. 8(1) / Art. 9(1) continuous monitoring discharges the financial entity's continuous-monitoring obligation and the GDPR detection capability becomes a sub-component within the DORA-led stack. The DORA Art. 10(1) prompt anomalous-activity detection + multi-layered controls + alert thresholds + auto-alert to relevant staff add the operational detection layer.

The **AI Act post-market monitoring feedback loop** is the structurally significant COMPLEMENTARY dimension: AI Act Art. 72(2) `actively and systematically collect, document and analyse relevant data` adds the feedback-loop dimension (analysis-to-risk-management loop) that none of the other 4 regulators impose in equivalent form. The AI Act post-market monitoring data feeds back into the AI-Act provider's risk-management-system documentation (Art. 9 — cross to D-09.1) and the AI-system quality management system (Art. 17 — cross to D-09.1).

The **NIS 2 partial coverage** is the structurally significant scope asymmetry: NIS 2 does not impose a dedicated continuous-monitoring mandate beyond the detection dimension of incident handling (Art. 21(2)(b)). The D-10.1 coverage on the NIS 2 side is partial via the SO-NIS2-005 incident-handling detection capability. For DORA financial entities that are also NSIEs, the DORA-led stack discharges the NIS 2 partial-coverage obligation; for non-financial NSIEs, the NIS 2 detection capability operates on the all-hazards basis without a dedicated continuous-monitoring mandate.

The **CRA opt-out mechanism** is the structurally significant policy divergence: CRA is the only regulator that explicitly permits user opt-out from monitoring (Annex I Part I (2)(l)). The other 4 regulators impose mandatory monitoring on the entity layer (GDPR / NIS 2 / DORA) or AI-system layer (AI Act). The opt-out mechanism is the origin of the 1 CRDA-flagged GENUINE TENSION that propagates to 3 of 10 pairs.

The high-level SO surfaces the common continuous-monitoring duty (pre-breach detection + on-device internal activity + ICT-system security and functioning + AI-system post-market data + incident-handling detection capability + cross-recipient evidence trail); the per-reg perspective on the monitoring mandate, anchor, scope dimension, trigger, threshold, recipient, and content template is documented separately in the 5 sub-SOs and the per-recipient evidence packs. **1 conditional emergent tension** (CRA opt-out vs. mandatory monitoring — Layer 2 OJ-level resolution required) is preserved as a Layer 2 review item because no OJ-level consolidation rule exists at D-10.1.

#### Downstream implication for Phase 1C Doc 07

A company with ≥2 applicable regulations in scope of D-10.1 — with overlapping monitoring-architecture scope-determiners on the same party — operates a **layered 5-monitoring-architecture** with 5 distinct monitoring mandates, 5 distinct scope layers, and 1 CRDA-flagged GENUINE TENSION requiring Layer 2 OJ-level resolution. The high-level SO (`SO-D-10.1.HL`) describes the common continuous-monitoring duty (pre-breach detection + on-device internal activity + ICT-system security and functioning + AI-system post-market data + incident-handling detection capability + cross-recipient evidence trail); the per-reg perspective is preserved in the 5 sub-SOs (mandate, scope layer, anchor, content template, threshold, recipient, evidence template). **The 1 conditional emergent tension is preserved as a Layer 2 OJ-level resolution item** — Phase 1C Doc 07 must surface the CRA Annex I Part I (2)(l) user opt-out vs. GDPR Art. 32(2) / NIS 2 Art. 21(2)(b) / DORA Art. 8(1) mandatory-monitoring conflict because no OJ-level consolidation rule exists at D-10.1. The high-level SO does NOT collapse the 5 monitoring mandates into a single monitoring duty; the per-reg perspective on the mandate, scope layer, anchor, content template, threshold, recipient, and evidence template is preserved verbatim. Phase 1C Doc 07 documents the **continuous-monitoring strategy**: (1) a 5-layer monitoring architecture with explicit scope-layer hand-offs (GDPR Art. 32(2) + Art. 33(1) entity-level 5-risk-type detection with breach-`becoming-aware` clock → NIS 2 Art. 21(2)(b) entity-level all-hazards detection capability as partial coverage → CRA Annex I Part I (2)(l) + Annex I Part II (6) product-level on-device internal-activity monitoring with user opt-out + vulnerability reporting → DORA Art. 8(1) per `SR-DORA-030` OJ-literal locus Art. 9(1) continuous monitoring of ICT-system security and functioning + Art. 10(1)+(2) prompt anomalous-activity detection + Art. 7(2) continuous ICT risk identification → AI Act Art. 72(1)–(2) + Art. 74 post-market monitoring with `proportionate to the nature of the AI technologies and the risks` qualitative proportionality + market-surveillance cooperation); (2) the 1 CRDA-flagged GENUINE TENSION (CRA Annex I Part I (2)(l) user opt-out vs. mandatory-monitoring regimes — GDPR Art. 32(2), NIS 2 Art. 21(2)(b), DORA Art. 8(1)) as a Layer 2 OJ-level resolution item propagating to 3 of 10 pairs (GDPR↔CRA primary, NIS2↔CRA, CRA↔DORA); (3) the NIS 2 ↔ DORA EQUAL classification (DORA-dominant for financial entities under DORA's direct scope — both impose continuous-monitoring obligations); (4) the DORA-led stack for DORA financial entities that are also controllers (DORA Art. 9(1) continuous monitoring discharges the financial entity's continuous-monitoring obligation and the GDPR detection capability becomes a sub-component); (5) the AI Act post-market monitoring feedback loop (Art. 72(2) `actively and systematically collect, document and analyse` 3-way verb AND feeds back into Art. 9 risk-management-system documentation + Art. 17 QMS); (6) the NIS 2 partial-coverage dimension (NIS 2 does not impose a dedicated continuous-monitoring mandate beyond the detection dimension of incident handling); (7) the CRA opt-out mechanism as the structurally significant policy divergence (CRA is the only regulator that explicitly permits user opt-out from monitoring); (8) the D-10.1 monitoring evidence feeds the D-04 incident-response chain (D-04.1 Detection & Triage, D-04.2 Containment & Mitigation, D-04.3 Regulatory Notification, D-04.4 Data Restoration & Recovery) and the D-09.4 Records of Processing documentation obligation as upstream inputs.

---
