**See also:** [`D-09.4.md`](../D-09_Governance-Documentation/D-09.4.md) (Records of Processing — adjacent documentation sub-domain); [`index.md`](../index.md); [`../../CrossRegulation/DeepAnalysis/D-09_Governance-Documentation/D-10.1.md`](../../CrossRegulation/DeepAnalysis/D-09_Governance-Documentation/D-10.1.md) (CRDA-deep source).

### 3. Security Requirements


## 10.1 Continuous Security Monitoring — Volere requirements

> **Provenance.** Derived from `../../HierarchicalSecurityObjectives/D-10_Monitoring-Audit/10.1.md`. See that file for the CRDA-deep verified classification (10 pairs: 9 SAME — COMPLEMENTARY + 1 SAME — EQUAL NIS 2 ↔ DORA on continuous-monitoring dimension, DORA-dominant for financial entities — no CORRECTED, no CONTRADICTORY, no scope-disjoint pairs; **5 of 5 regulations participate**), the **1 CRDA-flagged GENUINE TENSION (CRA Annex I Part I (2)(l) user opt-out vs. mandatory-monitoring regimes — GDPR Art. 32(2), NIS 2 Art. 21(2)(b), DORA Art. 8(1))** propagating to 3 of 10 pairs (GDPR↔CRA primary, NIS2↔CRA, CRA↔DORA) — the OJ does not resolve the conflict; **Layer 2 OJ-level resolution required**, the per-reg 5-distinct-monitoring-mandate architecture (GDPR Art. 32(2) 5-risk-type entity-level detection + Art. 33(1) breach-`becoming-aware` clock; NIS 2 Art. 21(2)(b) entity-level all-hazards detection capability as incident-handling dimension — partial coverage, no dedicated continuous-monitoring mandate; CRA Annex I Part I (2)(l) on-device internal-activity monitoring with user opt-out + Annex I Part II (6) vulnerability reporting; DORA Art. 8(1) per SR-DORA-030 OJ-literal locus Art. 9(1) continuous monitoring of ICT-system security and functioning + Art. 10(1)+(2) prompt anomalous-activity detection + Art. 7(2) continuous ICT risk identification; AI Act Art. 72(1)-(2) post-market monitoring `proportionate to the nature of the AI technologies and the risks` + Art. 74 market-surveillance cooperation), the **NIS 2 ↔ DORA EQUAL classification on continuous-monitoring dimension** (DORA-dominant for financial entities; both impose continuous-monitoring obligations; DORA Art. 9(1) discharges both DORA's dedicated monitoring obligation and NIS 2's Art. 21(2)(b) partial-coverage detection dimension), the **DORA-led stack for financial entities** (DORA Art. 8(1) continuous monitoring is broader than GDPR Art. 32(2); GDPR detection as sub-component within DORA-led stack), the **AI Act post-market monitoring feedback loop** (Art. 72(2) `actively and systematically collect, document and analyse` → analysis-to-risk-management loop feeding Art. 9 + Art. 17), the **CRA opt-out as structurally significant policy divergence** (CRA is the only regulator that explicitly permits user opt-out from monitoring), the per-reg monitoring mandate / scope layer / anchor / content template / threshold / recipient / evidence template, and the aggregation logic. This file adds Volere shell fields per the `../Volere_shell.md` template.

### Participating regulations

GDPR, NIS 2, CRA, DORA, AI Act (ALL 5 participate on the monitoring object). The five regulators each impose a **distinct monitoring mandate** at a **distinct scope layer** under a **distinct anchor**: GDPR (Art. 32(1)(b) `the ability to ensure the ongoing confidentiality, integrity, availability and resilience of processing systems and services` + Art. 32(1)(d) `a process for regularly testing, assessing and evaluating` + Art. 32(2) 5-risk-type detection (accidental or unlawful destruction, loss, alteration, unauthorised disclosure of, or access to personal data) + Art. 33(1) breach-`becoming-aware` clock `without undue delay and, where feasible, not later than 72 hours after having become aware of it`) — entity-level 5-risk-type detection on personal-data processing systems; data subject + DPA; NIS 2 (Art. 21(2)(b) `incident handling` 6-action lifecycle: prevent, detect, analyse, contain, respond, recover) — entity-level all-hazards detection capability as incident-handling dimension; **partial coverage, no dedicated continuous-monitoring mandate**; management body + competent authority (CSIRT); CRA (Annex I Part I (2)(l) `provide security-related information by recording and monitoring relevant internal activity, including access to or modification of data, services or functions, and provide an opt-out mechanism for the user` + Annex I Part II (6) `facilitate the sharing of information about potential vulnerabilities of the product with digital elements`) — product-level on-device internal-activity monitoring with **user opt-out** + vulnerability reporting; MSA + users; DORA (Art. 8(1) per SR-DORA-030 OJ-literal locus Art. 9(1) `Financial entities shall continuously monitor and control the security and functioning of ICT systems and tools and shall minimise the impact of ICT risk on ICT systems through the deployment of appropriate ICT security tools, policies and procedures` + Art. 10(1) `promptly detect anomalous activities` + Art. 7(2) continuous ICT risk identification on yearly review) — financial-entity continuous monitoring of ICT-system security and functioning + prompt anomalous-activity detection + continuous ICT risk identification; NCA + management body; AI Act (Art. 72(1) `Providers shall establish and document a post-market monitoring system in a manner that is proportionate to the nature of the AI technologies and the risks of the high-risk AI system` + Art. 72(2) `The post-market monitoring system shall actively and systematically collect, document and analyse relevant data` + Art. 74(1) market-surveillance cooperation under Regulation (EU) 2019/1020) — AI-system post-market monitoring; MSA + AI Office + provider.

---

### High-level requirement

```yaml
- req_id: 10.1
  volere_section: 15
  volere_subtype: audit
  title: "Continuous security monitoring established through a layered 5-monitoring-architecture with 5 distinct monitoring mandates at 5 distinct scope layers, with 1 CRDA-flagged GENUINE TENSION on CRA user opt-out vs. mandatory monitoring requiring Layer 2 OJ-level resolution"

  derives_from:
    hso_sub_so: SO-10.1.HL
    hso_file: ../../HierarchicalSecurityObjectives/D-10_Monitoring-Audit/10.1.md
    objective_ref_section: "### 10.1.0"

  description: |
    The product shall establish continuous security monitoring through a layered 5-monitoring-architecture with 5 distinct monitoring mandates (GDPR 5-risk-type entity-level detection + NIS 2 all-hazards detection capability + CRA on-device internal-activity with user opt-out + DORA continuous ICT-system monitoring + AI Act AI-system post-market monitoring) at 5 distinct scope layers (entity personal-data + entity operational + product on-device + entity ICT-system + AI-system lifecycle), where ≥2 of the participating regulations (GDPR, NIS 2, CRA, DORA, AI Act) have applicability with overlapping monitoring-architecture scope-determiners on the same party, with the per-reg monitoring mandate, scope layer, anchor, content template, threshold, recipient, and evidence template preserved verbatim in the 5 sub-SOs, and with the 1 CRDA-flagged GENUINE TENSION (CRA user opt-out vs. mandatory monitoring) escalated to Layer 2 OJ-level resolution.

  fit_criterion: |
    A layered 5-monitoring-architecture is established with explicit scope-layer hand-offs at the monitoring-evidence layer: (1) **GDPR Art. 32(1)(b) + Art. 32(1)(d) + Art. 32(2) + Art. 33(1)** — entity-level monitoring of personal-data processing systems for the 5 Art. 32(2) risk types (accidental or unlawful destruction, loss, alteration, unauthorised disclosure of, or access to personal data) + Art. 32(1)(d) regular testing/assessing/evaluating + Art. 33(1) breach-`becoming-aware` clock `without undue delay and, where feasible, not later than 72 hours after having become aware of it`; data subject + DPA recipients; (2) **NIS 2 Art. 21(2)(b)** — entity-level all-hazards detection capability as incident-handling dimension with the 6-action lifecycle (prevent, detect, analyse, contain, respond, recover); **partial coverage — no dedicated continuous-monitoring mandate**; management body + competent authority (CSIRT) recipients; (3) **CRA Annex I Part I (2)(l) + Annex I Part II (6)** — product-level on-device internal-activity monitoring of access to or modification of data, services, or functions with **user opt-out mechanism** + vulnerability-reporting contact address; MSA + users recipients; (4) **DORA Art. 8(1) per SR-DORA-030 OJ-literal locus Art. 9(1) + Art. 10(1) + Art. 10(2) + Art. 7(2)** — financial-entity continuous monitoring of ICT-system security AND functioning + prompt anomalous-activity detection through multi-layered controls + alert thresholds + identification of single points of failure + continuous ICT risk identification on yearly review; NCA + management body recipients; (5) **AI Act Art. 72(1) + Art. 72(2) + Art. 72(3) + Art. 74(1)** — AI-system post-market monitoring `actively and systematically collect, document and analyse` 3-way verb AND + post-market monitoring plan + Art. 74(1) market-surveillance cooperation under Regulation (EU) 2019/1020; MSA + AI Office + provider recipients. The 5 mandates are stacked on distinct scope layers; the per-reg anchor, content template, threshold, recipient, and evidence template are preserved verbatim. **NIS 2 ↔ DORA EQUAL** on continuous-monitoring dimension — for DORA financial entities, the DORA Art. 9(1) continuous monitoring discharges both DORA's dedicated monitoring obligation and NIS 2's Art. 21(2)(b) partial-coverage detection dimension (DORA-dominant for financial entities under DORA's direct scope). **DORA-led stack for financial entities** — for DORA financial entities that are also controllers, the DORA Art. 8(1) / Art. 9(1) continuous monitoring is broader (security AND functioning) than GDPR Art. 32(2) 5-risk-type; GDPR detection becomes a sub-component within the DORA-led stack. **CRDA-flagged GENUINE TENSION** — CRA Annex I Part I (2)(l) user opt-out vs. mandatory-monitoring regimes (GDPR Art. 32(2), NIS 2 Art. 21(2)(b), DORA Art. 8(1)) — the OJ does not resolve the conflict; **Layer 2 OJ-level resolution required**; the two regimes stack on the layered monitoring architecture (entity-layer mandatory + product-layer with opt-out), but the user's opt-out from CRA monitoring does not legally exempt the controller / NSIE / DORA entity from the respective mandatory-monitoring regime. Verified by per-recipient evidence packs (DPA monitoring evidence + breach-`becoming-aware` clock; CSIRT incident-handling evidence; MSA + users Annex I Part I (2)(l) + Annex I Part II (6) evidence; NCA + management body DORA Art. 8(1) / Art. 10(1) evidence; MSA + AI Office + provider Art. 72(1)-(3) + Art. 74(1) evidence) + automated cross-scope-layer hand-off test (each monitoring mandate activates on the correct scope layer; the DORA-led stack subsumes the GDPR detection capability for DORA financial entities; the CRA user opt-out is recorded but does not block entity-layer mandatory monitoring) + Layer 2 OJ-level resolution log documenting the CRA opt-out vs. mandatory-monitoring conflict.

  priority: MUST
  verification_method: TEST
  dependencies: [10.1.1, 10.1.2, 10.1.3, 10.1.4, 10.1.5]

  nist_csf: [DE.CM-01, DE.CM-09, DE.AE-02, ID.IM-04]

  applicable_if:
    regs: [GDPR, NIS2, CRA, DORA, AI_Act]
    scope_overlap: Y   # HL engages whenever ≥2 applicable regs have ≥1 Y or Conditional-Y-scope-overlap pair on the same party; for 10.1 the matrix shows every pair has Y or Conditional-Y activation on the joint-condition operator (no N (typically) scope-disjoint pairs), so the HL engages whenever ≥2 regs are applicable. The 9 SAME — COMPLEMENTARY pairs stack on the layered 5-monitoring-architecture; the 1 SAME — EQUAL pair (NIS 2 ↔ DORA) discharges both obligations on the DORA-dominant continuous-monitoring stack for financial entities. The 1 CRDA-flagged GENUINE TENSION (CRA user opt-out vs. mandatory monitoring) propagates to 3 of 10 pairs (GDPR↔CRA primary, NIS2↔CRA, CRA↔DORA); the HL does NOT resolve the tension — it is preserved as a conditional emergent tension requiring Layer 2 OJ-level resolution. The HL does NOT collapse the 5 monitoring mandates into a single monitoring duty; the per-reg perspective on the mandate, scope layer, anchor, content template, threshold, recipient, and evidence template is preserved verbatim in the 5 sub-SOs.

  satisfaction:
    controls: []
    nodes: []

  considerations: |
    The high-level SO surfaces the **common continuous-monitoring duty** (pre-breach detection + on-device internal activity + ICT-system security and functioning + AI-system post-market data + incident-handling detection capability + cross-recipient evidence trail). The 10 verified pairs are all SAME — 9 SAME — COMPLEMENTARY + 1 SAME — EQUAL (NIS 2 ↔ DORA on continuous-monitoring dimension, DORA-dominant for financial entities) — no CORRECTED, no CONTRADICTORY, no scope-disjoint pairs. **1 CRDA-flagged GENUINE TENSION — Layer 2 OJ-level resolution required**: CRA Annex I Part I (2)(l) user opt-out vs. mandatory-monitoring regimes (GDPR Art. 32(2), NIS 2 Art. 21(2)(b), DORA Art. 8(1)) — the OJ does not resolve the conflict; the tension propagates to 3 of 10 pairs (GDPR↔CRA primary, NIS2↔CRA, CRA↔DORA). Phase 1 entities with this conflict must (a) maintain the mandatory monitoring under GDPR / NIS 2 / DORA on the entity layer regardless of the CRA opt-out at the product layer, and (b) escalate to Layer 2 OJ-level resolution for the cross-layer conflict. The two regimes can stack on the layered monitoring architecture (entity-layer mandatory + product-layer with opt-out), but the user's opt-out from CRA monitoring may not legally exempt the controller / NSIE / DORA entity from the respective mandatory-monitoring regime. **NIS 2 ↔ DORA EQUAL** discharges both on the DORA-dominant stack for financial entities. **DORA-led stack for financial entities** — DORA Art. 8(1) continuous monitoring is broader (security AND functioning) than GDPR Art. 32(2) 5-risk-type; for DORA financial entities that are also controllers, the DORA Art. 8(1) / Art. 9(1) continuous monitoring discharges the financial entity's continuous-monitoring obligation and the GDPR detection capability becomes a sub-component within the DORA-led stack. **AI Act post-market monitoring feedback loop** (Art. 72(2) `actively and systematically collect, document and analyse` → analysis-to-risk-management loop feeding Art. 9 + Art. 17) is AI-Act-specific and is preserved in the AI Act sub-SO. **NIS 2 partial coverage** — NIS 2 does not impose a dedicated continuous-monitoring mandate beyond the detection dimension of incident handling (Art. 21(2)(b)); the 10.1 coverage on the NIS 2 side is partial via the SO-NIS2-005 incident-handling detection capability. **CRA opt-out mechanism** — CRA is the only regulator that explicitly permits user opt-out from monitoring (Annex I Part I (2)(l)); the opt-out mechanism is the origin of the 1 CRDA-flagged GENUINE TENSION. The HL does NOT collapse the 5 monitoring mandates into a single monitoring duty; the per-reg perspective on the mandate, scope layer, anchor, content template, threshold, recipient, and evidence template is preserved verbatim.
```

---

### Sub-requirements

#### 10.1.1 — Entity-level 5 Art. 32(2) risk-type detection with breach-`becoming-aware` clock anchoring the Art. 33(1) 72-hour notification

```yaml
- req_id: 10.1.1
  volere_section: 15
  volere_subtype: audit

  title: "Controller and processor monitor personal-data processing systems for the 5 Art. 32(2) risk types, with breach-`becoming-aware` clock anchoring the Art. 33(1) 72-hour notification"

  derives_from:
    hso_sub_so: SO-10.1.GDPR
    per_reg_so: SO-GDPR-030 + SO-GDPR-008
    source_sr: SR-GDPR-019 + SR-GDPR-020 + SR-GDPR-021
    hso_file: ../../HierarchicalSecurityObjectives/D-10_Monitoring-Audit/10.1.md
    objective_ref_section: "### 10.1.1"

  description: |
    The product shall implement appropriate technical and organisational measures to ensure the ongoing confidentiality, integrity, availability and resilience of processing systems and services (per GDPR Art. 32(1)(b)) and regularly test, assess and evaluate their effectiveness (per Art. 32(1)(d) `a process for regularly testing, assessing and evaluating`), with personal-data processing systems and services monitored for the 5 Art. 32(2) risk types (accidental or unlawful destruction, loss, alteration, unauthorised disclosure of, or access to personal data), and a clear definition of the moment of `becoming aware of` a breach to anchor the Art. 33(1) 72-hour notification clock `without undue delay and, where feasible, not later than 72 hours after having become aware of it`.

  fit_criterion: |
    Entity-level monitoring of personal-data processing systems is operational per Art. 32(1)(b) + Art. 32(1)(d) + Art. 32(2) + Art. 33(1) (SR-GDPR-019 + SR-GDPR-020 + SR-GDPR-021) verified by DE.CM-01 primary (Networks and network services are monitored to find potentially adverse events — the controller demonstrates ex post that the 5-risk-type monitoring is operational on personal-data processing systems per DomainAnalysis cross-validation) + DE.CM-09 (Computing hardware and software, runtime environments, and their data are monitored — anchors the personal-data processing systems monitoring dimension) + DE.AE-02 (Adverse events are analyzed to identify or determine the scope of an incident — anchors the breach-`becoming-aware` clock per Art. 33(1) `the controller shall without undue delay and, where feasible, not later than 72 hours after having become aware of it, notify the personal data breach to the supervisory authority`) + ID.IM-04 (Adequate resource capacity to ensure availability is maintained — anchors the Art. 32(1)(b) ongoing availability dimension); the 5 Art. 32(2) risk types are: (i) accidental or unlawful destruction, (ii) loss, (iii) alteration, (iv) unauthorised disclosure of, (v) access to personal data; the breach-`becoming-aware` clock is operationalised with a clear definition of the moment of `becoming aware of` (per EDPB Guidelines 9/2022 on personal data breach notification under Regulation 2016/679 §3.2 — the controller has a high degree of certainty that a security incident has occurred that has led to personal data being compromised) and an audit trail that documents the moment-of-becoming-aware timestamp + the Art. 33(1) 72-hour notification timestamp; the recipient is the **data subject + DPA (supervisory authority)** on the Art. 32(2) detection evidence + Art. 33(1) notification; verified by DPA audit pack (5-risk-type monitoring evidence, breach-`becoming-aware` clock evidence, Art. 33(1) notification log, Art. 5(2) accountability burden evidence) + automated 5-risk-type coverage test (each of the 5 risk types is covered by the monitoring evidence) + automated breach-`becoming-aware` clock test (Art. 33(1) 72-hour notification triggered within 72h of moment-of-becoming-aware). **For DORA financial entities**, the **DORA Art. 8(1) per SR-DORA-030 OJ-literal locus Art. 9(1) continuous monitoring is broader (security AND functioning) than GDPR Art. 32(2) 5-risk-type detection; the DORA-led stack discharges the financial entity's continuous-monitoring obligation and the GDPR detection capability becomes a sub-component within the DORA-led stack (DORA-led stack for financial entities — cross to 10.1.4).

  priority: MUST
  verification_method: TEST
  dependencies: [10.1.2 (when controller = NSIE — GDPR detection capability + NIS 2 all-hazards detection capability on overlapping detection capability with different triggering frameworks — risk-type-triggered vs. incident-triggered; partial trigger alignment), 10.1.3 (when controller-manufacturer — **CRDA-flagged GENUINE TENSION**: CRA user opt-out vs. GDPR mandatory monitoring; the OJ does not resolve; Layer 2 OJ-level resolution required; the two regimes stack on the layered monitoring architecture — entity-layer mandatory + product-layer with opt-out), 10.1.4 (when DORA-controller — DORA Art. 8(1) / Art. 9(1) continuous monitoring is broader than GDPR Art. 32(2); DORA-led stack with GDPR detection as sub-component; for DORA financial entities, the DORA-led stack discharges the GDPR continuous-monitoring obligation), 10.1.5 (when provider/deployer = controller — GDPR pre-breach detection + AI Act lifecycle monitoring on different trigger alignment; pre-breach vs. lifecycle; the two regimes stack)]

  nist_csf: [DE.CM-01, DE.CM-09, DE.AE-02, ID.IM-04]

  applicable_if:
    regs: [GDPR]
    scope_overlap: Y   # GDPR↔NIS2 = Y (controller = NSIE; detection capability overlaps with different triggering frameworks); GDPR↔CRA = Y (CRDA-flagged GENUINE TENSION — controller-manufacturer; CRA user opt-out vs. GDPR mandatory monitoring; conflict requires Layer 2 OJ-level resolution); GDPR↔DORA = Y (DORA financial entity = controller; DORA-led stack with GDPR 5-risk-type as sub-component); GDPR↔AI_Act = Conditional (Y when high-risk AI processes personal data — provider is controller); HL engages when the GDPR sub-SO activates AND ≥1 other sub-SO activates on Y or Conditional-Y overlap.

  satisfaction:
    controls: []
    nodes: []

  considerations: |
    The GDPR continuous-monitoring obligation at 10.1 is the **entity-level 5 Art. 32(2) risk-type detection** + **breach-`becoming-aware` clock** anchoring the Art. 33(1) 72-hour notification. Art. 32(1)(b) `the ability to ensure the ongoing confidentiality, integrity, availability and resilience of processing systems and services` + Art. 32(1)(d) `a process for regularly testing, assessing and evaluating` + Art. 32(2) 5-risk-type trigger `the ability to restore the availability and access to personal data in a timely manner in the event of a physical or technical incident` (chapeau) + Art. 33(1) `the controller shall without undue delay and, where feasible, not later than 72 hours after having become aware of it, notify the personal data breach to the supervisory authority` (per SR-GDPR-019 + SR-GDPR-020 + SR-GDPR-021). The 5 Art. 32(2) risk types: (i) accidental or unlawful destruction, (ii) loss, (iii) alteration, (iv) unauthorised disclosure of, (v) access to personal data. The `becoming aware` clock is operationalised per EDPB Guidelines 9/2022 §3.2 — the controller has a high degree of certainty that a security incident has occurred that has led to personal data being compromised; the `without undue delay and, where feasible, not later than 72 hours` is a dual qualifier — `without undue delay` is immediate, `where feasible, not later than 72 hours` is the soft ceiling; the 72-hour clock starts at the moment-of-becoming-aware and ends at the DPA notification. The recipient is the **data subject + DPA (supervisory authority)** on the Art. 32(2) detection evidence + Art. 33(1) notification. **For DORA financial entities**, the DORA Art. 8(1) / Art. 9(1) continuous monitoring is broader than GDPR Art. 32(2) 5-risk-type; the DORA-led stack discharges the financial entity's continuous-monitoring obligation and the GDPR detection capability becomes a sub-component within the DORA-led stack. **CRDA-flagged GENUINE TENSION with CRA**: CRA Annex I Part I (2)(l) user opt-out vs. GDPR Art. 32(2) mandatory monitoring — Layer 2 OJ-level resolution required.
```

#### 10.1.2 — Entity-level all-hazards detection capability as incident-handling dimension with 6-action lifecycle (partial coverage)

```yaml
- req_id: 10.1.2
  volere_section: 15
  volere_subtype: audit

  title: "Essential and important entities establish an incident-handling capability with 6-action lifecycle (prevent, detect, analyse, contain, respond, recover) covering detection of all-hazards incidents"

  derives_from:
    hso_sub_so: SO-10.1.NIS2
    per_reg_so: SO-NIS2-005
    source_sr: SR-NIS2-009 + SR-NIS2-014
    hso_file: ../../HierarchicalSecurityObjectives/D-10_Monitoring-Audit/10.1.md
    objective_ref_section: "### 10.1.2"

  description: |
    The product shall establish an incident-handling capability covering prevention, detection, analysis, containment, response, and recovery of incidents (per NIS 2 Art. 21(2)(b) `incident handling`), including those with significant impact on the provision of services, with the detection dimension operating on an all-hazards basis and feeding the Art. 23(1) significant-incident notification chain; the Art. 21(2)(b) detection capability is the **partial-coverage dimension** of 10.1 — NIS 2 does not impose a dedicated continuous-monitoring mandate beyond the detection dimension of incident handling.

  fit_criterion: |
    An incident-handling capability is established per Art. 21(2)(b) `incident handling` (SR-NIS2-009 + SR-NIS2-014) with the 6-action lifecycle (prevent, detect, analyse, contain, respond, recover — per SR-NIS2-008) verified by DE.CM-01 primary (Networks and network services are monitored to find potentially adverse events — anchors the NIS 2 Art. 21(2)(b) incident-handling detection capability mapped to NIST CSF 2.0 DE.CM-01) + DE.CM-09 (Computing hardware and software, runtime environments, and their data are monitored — anchors the all-hazards network and information systems monitoring per DomainAnalysis cross-validation) + DE.AE-02 (Adverse events are analyzed to identify or determine the scope of an incident — anchors the Art. 23(1) significant-incident detection trigger) + ID.IM-04 (Adequate resource capacity — anchors the prevention+detection+response feedback loop); the detection dimension operates on an all-hazards basis (all sources of ICT risk + cyber threats + ICT vulnerabilities — Art. 21(1) chapeau `Member States shall ensure that essential and important entities take appropriate and proportionate technical, operational and organisational measures to manage the risks posed to the security of network and information systems … which those entities use for their activities or the provision of their services, and to prevent or minimise the impact of incidents on the recipients of their services`); the all-hazards detection capability feeds the Art. 23(1) significant-incident notification chain (cross to 4.3 Regulatory Notification) when an incident has a significant impact on the continuity of essential services; the recipient is the **management body + competent authority (CSIRT — Computer Security Incident Response Team or national competent authority)** on the Art. 21(2)(b) detection capability + incident-handling 6-action lifecycle; verified by CSIRT audit pack (incident-handling 6-action lifecycle evidence, Art. 21(2)(b) all-hazards detection capability, Art. 23(1) significant-incident notification chain). **IMPORTANT — NIS 2 partial coverage at 10.1**: NIS 2 does NOT impose a dedicated continuous-monitoring mandate beyond the detection dimension of incident handling (Art. 21(2)(b)); the 10.1 coverage on the NIS 2 side is partial via the SO-NIS2-005 incident-handling detection capability. For DORA financial entities under DORA's direct scope, the **DORA Art. 8(1) per SR-DORA-030 OJ-literal locus Art. 9(1) continuous monitoring is the dominant authority** and discharges both DORA's dedicated monitoring obligation and NIS 2's Art. 21(2)(b) partial-coverage detection dimension for DORA financial entities that are also NSIEs — **NIS 2 ↔ DORA EQUAL on continuous-monitoring dimension, DORA-dominant for financial entities**; for non-financial NSIEs, NIS 2 Art. 21(2)(b) detection capability operates on the all-hazards basis without a dedicated continuous-monitoring mandate.

  priority: MUST
  verification_method: TEST
  dependencies: [10.1.1 (when controller = NSIE — GDPR detection capability + NIS 2 all-hazards detection capability on overlapping detection capability with different triggering frameworks — risk-type-triggered vs. incident-triggered; partial trigger alignment), 10.1.3 (when NSIE deploys CRA-regulated products — **CRDA-flagged GENUINE TENSION propagated**: CRA user opt-out vs. NIS 2 mandatory monitoring; the OJ does not resolve; Layer 2 OJ-level resolution required; the two regimes stack on the layered monitoring architecture — entity-layer mandatory + product-layer with opt-out), 10.1.4 (when DORA-NSIE — **NIS 2 ↔ DORA EQUAL on continuous-monitoring dimension, DORA-dominant for financial entities**; DORA Art. 8(1) / Art. 9(1) continuous monitoring discharges both DORA's dedicated monitoring obligation and NIS 2's Art. 21(2)(b) partial-coverage detection dimension; for non-financial NSIEs, NIS 2 detection capability operates on the all-hazards basis without DORA), 10.1.5 (when NSIE deploys high-risk AI — NIS 2 entity-level operational monitoring + AI Act AI-system lifecycle monitoring; different trigger alignment; the two regimes stack without overlap)]

  nist_csf: [DE.CM-01, DE.CM-09, DE.AE-02, ID.IM-04]

  applicable_if:
    regs: [NIS2]
    scope_overlap: Y   # NIS2↔GDPR = Y (controller = NSIE; detection capability overlaps); NIS2↔CRA = Conditional (Y when NSIE deploys CRA-regulated products; **CRDA-flagged GENUINE TENSION propagated** — CRA user opt-out vs. NIS 2 mandatory monitoring); NIS2↔DORA = Y (DORA financial entity = NSIE; **EQUAL on continuous-monitoring dimension, DORA-dominant for financial entities**); NIS2↔AI_Act = Conditional (Y when NSIE deploys high-risk AI); HL engages when the NIS 2 sub-SO activates AND ≥1 other sub-SO activates on Y or Conditional-Y overlap.

  satisfaction:
    controls: []
    nodes: []

  considerations: |
    The NIS 2 continuous-monitoring obligation at 10.1 is the **entity-level all-hazards detection capability** as incident-handling dimension per Art. 21(2)(b) — **partial coverage, no dedicated continuous-monitoring mandate**. The 6-action incident-handling lifecycle per SR-NIS2-008 (prevent, detect, analyse, contain, respond, recover) is the operational backbone; the detection dimension is the partial-coverage dimension of 10.1 on the NIS 2 side. Art. 21(2)(b) `incident handling` (per SR-NIS2-009) is the partial-coverage obligation; the all-hazards basis derives from Art. 21(1) chapeau `appropriate and proportionate technical, operational and organisational measures to manage the risks posed to the security of network and information systems`. The detection capability feeds the Art. 23(1) significant-incident notification chain (cross to 4.3 Regulatory Notification) when an incident has a significant impact on the continuity of essential services. The recipient is the **management body + competent authority (CSIRT or national competent authority)** on the Art. 21(2)(b) detection capability. **For DORA financial entities that are also NSIEs**, the **DORA Art. 8(1) / Art. 9(1) continuous monitoring discharges both obligations** — DORA-dominant. **CRDA-flagged GENUINE TENSION with CRA (propagated)**: CRA user opt-out vs. NIS 2 mandatory monitoring — Layer 2 OJ-level resolution required.
```

#### 10.1.3 — Product-level on-device internal-activity monitoring with user opt-out + vulnerability-reporting mechanism

```yaml
- req_id: 10.1.3
  volere_section: 15
  volere_subtype: audit

  title: "Product with digital elements records and monitors relevant internal activity (access to or modification of data, services or functions) with a user opt-out mechanism, and enables vulnerability sharing through a single contact address"

  derives_from:
    hso_sub_so: SO-10.1.CRA
    per_reg_so: SO-CRA-020 + SO-CRA-068
    source_sr: SR-CRA-100 + SR-CRA-101
    hso_file: ../../HierarchicalSecurityObjectives/D-10_Monitoring-Audit/10.1.md
    objective_ref_section: "### 10.1.3"

  description: |
    The product shall provide security-related information by recording and monitoring relevant internal activity, including access to or modification of data, services or functions, and provide an opt-out mechanism for the user (per CRA Annex I Part I (2)(l) `provide security-related information by recording and monitoring relevant internal activity, including access to or modification of data, services or functions, and provide an opt-out mechanism for the user`); the manufacturer shall facilitate the sharing of information about potential vulnerabilities of the product with digital elements through a single contact address (per Annex I Part II (6) `facilitate the sharing of information about potential vulnerabilities of the product with digital elements`).

  fit_criterion: |
    Product-level on-device internal-activity monitoring is operational per Annex I Part I (2)(l) + Annex I Part II (6) (SR-CRA-100 + SR-CRA-101) verified by DE.CM-01 primary (Networks and network services are monitored to find potentially adverse events — anchors the CRA Annex I Part I (2)(l) on-device internal-activity monitoring mapped to NIST CSF 2.0 DE.CM-01) + DE.CM-09 (Computing hardware and software, runtime environments, and their data are monitored — anchors the product-level on-device monitoring per DomainAnalysis cross-validation) + ID.IM-04 (Adequate resource capacity — anchors the manufacturer-side observability dimension); the on-device monitoring records and monitors access to or modification of data, services, or functions; the user opt-out mechanism is operational with a clear opt-out interface (UI surface) + opt-out state preservation across product restarts; the vulnerability-reporting contact address is operational per Annex I Part II (6) — single contact address (email or web form) for the sharing of information about potential vulnerabilities of the product with digital elements, integrated with the manufacturer's CVD policy per Annex I Part II §5 (cross to 2.3 Vulnerability Management + 9.1 CRA sub-SO); the recipient is the **MSA (market surveillance authority) + users** on the Annex I Part I (2)(l) on-device monitoring with user opt-out + Annex I Part II (6) vulnerability-reporting contact address; verified by MSA audit pack (on-device monitoring evidence, user opt-out mechanism evidence, vulnerability-reporting contact address operational evidence) + automated on-device monitoring coverage test (access to data + access to services + access to functions + modification of data + modification of services + modification of functions are all monitored) + automated user opt-out mechanism test (opt-out interface accessible + opt-out state preserved across product restarts) + automated vulnerability-reporting contact address test (single contact address operational and responsive). **IMPORTANT — CRDA-flagged GENUINE TENSION (primary flag, GDPR↔CRA)**: CRA Annex I Part I (2)(l) user opt-out vs. mandatory-monitoring regimes (GDPR Art. 32(2), NIS 2 Art. 21(2)(b), DORA Art. 8(1)) — the OJ does not resolve this conflict. The tension propagates to 3 of 10 pairs (GDPR↔CRA primary, NIS2↔CRA propagated, CRA↔DORA propagated). Phase 1 entities with this conflict must (a) maintain the mandatory monitoring under GDPR / NIS 2 / DORA on the entity layer regardless of the CRA opt-out at the product layer, and (b) escalate to Layer 2 OJ-level resolution for the cross-layer conflict. The two regimes can stack on the layered monitoring architecture (entity-layer mandatory + product-layer with opt-out), but the user's opt-out from CRA monitoring does not legally exempt the controller / NSIE / DORA entity from the respective mandatory-monitoring regime. **IMPORTANT — CRA opt-out mechanism is the origin of the 1 CRDA-flagged GENUINE TENSION**: CRA is the only regulator that explicitly permits user opt-out from monitoring (Annex I Part I (2)(l)); the opt-out mechanism is structurally significant and propagates against any mandatory-monitoring regime.

  priority: MUST
  verification_method: TEST
  dependencies: [10.1.1 (when controller-manufacturer — **CRDA-flagged GENUINE TENSION (primary)**: CRA user opt-out vs. GDPR mandatory monitoring; the OJ does not resolve; Layer 2 OJ-level resolution required; the two regimes stack on the layered monitoring architecture — entity-layer mandatory + product-layer with opt-out), 10.1.2 (when NSIE deploys CRA-regulated products — **CRDA-flagged GENUINE TENSION (propagated)**: CRA user opt-out vs. NIS 2 mandatory monitoring; same structural conflict as GDPR↔CRA), 10.1.4 (when DORA entity deploys CRA-regulated products — **CRDA-flagged GENUINE TENSION (propagated)**: CRA user opt-out vs. DORA mandatory monitoring; same structural conflict as GDPR↔CRA), 10.1.5 (when CRA-regulated product is also high-risk AI — CRA Annex I conformity + AI Act post-market monitoring stack for AI-enabled products; the AI Act adds the feedback-loop dimension; the CRA opt-out tension does NOT extend to the AI Act pair — the AI Act Art. 72 monitoring is provider-side, not subject to user opt-out)]

  nist_csf: [DE.CM-01, DE.CM-09, ID.IM-04]

  applicable_if:
    regs: [CRA]
    scope_overlap: Conditional   # CRA↔GDPR = Y (**CRDA-flagged GENUINE TENSION** — controller-manufacturer; CRA opt-out vs. GDPR mandatory monitoring; conflict requires Layer 2 OJ-level resolution); CRA↔NIS2 = Conditional (Y when NSIE deploys CRA-regulated products; **CRDA-flagged GENUINE TENSION propagated**); CRA↔DORA = Conditional (Y when DORA entity deploys CRA-regulated products; **CRDA-flagged GENUINE TENSION propagated**); CRA↔AI_Act = Conditional (Y when CRA-regulated product is also high-risk AI; CRA Annex I conformity + AI Act Art. 72 post-market monitoring stack — no OJ-level consolidation; the CRA opt-out tension does NOT extend to the AI Act pair); HL engages when the CRA sub-SO activates AND ≥1 other sub-SO activates on Y or Conditional-Y overlap.

  satisfaction:
    controls: []
    nodes: []

  considerations: |
    The CRA continuous-monitoring obligation at 10.1 is the **product-level on-device internal-activity monitoring** with **user opt-out** + **vulnerability-reporting mechanism** per Annex I Part I (2)(l) + Annex I Part II (6). Annex I Part I (2)(l) `provide security-related information by recording and monitoring relevant internal activity, including access to or modification of data, services or functions, and provide an opt-out mechanism for the user` (per SR-CRA-100) — the `opt-out mechanism for the user` clause is VAG-3 (Berry §5.1 — OJ does not anchor the opt-out mechanism's form factor or scope); chosen reading: opt-out interface (UI surface) + opt-out state preservation across product restarts. The monitoring scope covers access to or modification of data, services, or functions. Annex I Part II (6) `facilitate the sharing of information about potential vulnerabilities of the product with digital elements` (per SR-CRA-101) — the vulnerability-reporting contact address is integrated with the manufacturer's CVD policy per Annex I Part II §5 (cross to 2.3 + 9.1). The recipient is the **MSA (market surveillance authority) + users** on the Annex I Part I (2)(l) on-device monitoring with user opt-out + Annex I Part II (6) vulnerability-reporting contact address. **CRDA-flagged GENUINE TENSION (primary flag, GDPR↔CRA)**: CRA user opt-out vs. mandatory-monitoring regimes (GDPR Art. 32(2), NIS 2 Art. 21(2)(b), DORA Art. 8(1)) — the OJ does not resolve this conflict. The tension propagates to 3 of 10 pairs (GDPR↔CRA primary, NIS2↔CRA propagated, CRA↔DORA propagated). Phase 1 entities with this conflict must (a) maintain the mandatory monitoring under GDPR / NIS 2 / DORA on the entity layer regardless of the CRA opt-out at the product layer, and (b) escalate to Layer 2 OJ-level resolution. The two regimes can stack on the layered monitoring architecture (entity-layer mandatory + product-layer with opt-out), but the user's opt-out from CRA monitoring does not legally exempt the controller / NSIE / DORA entity from the respective mandatory-monitoring regime.
```

#### 10.1.4 — Financial-entity continuous monitoring of ICT-system security and functioning + prompt anomalous-activity detection + continuous ICT risk identification (DORA-led stack)

```yaml
- req_id: 10.1.4
  volere_section: 15
  volere_subtype: audit

  title: "Financial entity continuously monitors and controls the security and functioning of ICT systems and tools, with prompt anomalous-activity detection through multi-layered controls and continuous ICT risk identification"

  derives_from:
    hso_sub_so: SO-10.1.DORA
    per_reg_so: SO-DORA-018 + SO-DORA-007 + SO-DORA-003
    source_sr: SR-DORA-030 + SR-DORA-011 + SR-DORA-012 + SR-DORA-004
    hso_file: ../../HierarchicalSecurityObjectives/D-10_Monitoring-Audit/10.1.md
    objective_ref_section: "### 10.1.4"

  description: |
    The product shall continuously monitor and control the security and functioning of ICT systems and tools and minimise the impact of ICT risk on ICT systems through the deployment of appropriate ICT security tools, policies and procedures (per DORA Art. 8(1) per SR-DORA-030; OJ-literal locus Art. 9(1) `continuously monitor and control the security and functioning of ICT systems and tools`), with anomalous activities promptly detected through multi-layered controls, alert thresholds, incident response triggers, and identification of single points of failure (per Art. 10(1) `promptly detect anomalous activities` + Art. 10(2) multiple layers + alert thresholds + auto-alert to relevant staff), and sources of ICT risk identified on a continuous basis and risk scenarios reviewed on a regular basis, at least yearly (per Art. 7(2)).

  fit_criterion: |
    Continuous monitoring of ICT systems and tools is operational per Art. 8(1) per SR-DORA-030 OJ-literal locus Art. 9(1) `Financial entities shall continuously monitor and control the security and functioning of ICT systems and tools and shall minimise the impact of ICT risk on ICT systems through the deployment of appropriate ICT security tools, policies and procedures` (SR-DORA-030) verified by DE.CM-01 primary (Networks and network services are monitored to find potentially adverse events — anchors the DORA Art. 8(1) `continuously monitor and control` mapped to NIST CSF 2.0 DE.CM-01) + DE.CM-09 (Computing hardware and software, runtime environments, and their data are monitored — anchors the financial-entity ICT-systems monitoring per DomainAnalysis cross-validation) + PR.PS-04 (Software installation, operation, and updates are performed consistent with the security policy — anchors the Art. 8(1) `tools, policies and procedures` 3-way AND) + DE.AE-02 (Adverse events are analyzed to identify or determine the scope of an incident — anchors the Art. 10(1) prompt anomalous-activity detection) + ID.RA-01 (Asset vulnerabilities are identified, validated, and recorded — anchors the Art. 7(2) continuous ICT risk identification + yearly review) + ID.RA-03 (Threats to assets are identified and recorded — anchors the Art. 7(2) `cyber threats and ICT vulnerabilities`); the Art. 10(1) + Art. 10(2) prompt anomalous-activity detection is operational through multi-layered controls (network layer + system layer + application layer + data layer), alert thresholds, identification of single points of failure, and auto-alert to relevant staff; the Art. 7(2) continuous ICT risk identification is operational on a continuous basis with risk scenarios reviewed on a regular basis, at least yearly; the recipient is the **NCA (national competent authority) + management body** on the Art. 8(1) continuous monitoring + Art. 10(1)+(2) multi-layered detection + Art. 7(2) continuous ICT risk identification; verified by NCA + EBA Guidelines supervisory baseline audit pack (continuous monitoring evidence, prompt anomalous-activity detection evidence with multi-layered controls, alert thresholds, identification of single points of failure, auto-alert evidence, Art. 7(2) continuous ICT risk identification evidence with yearly review documentation). **DORA-led stack for financial entities**: DORA Art. 8(1) continuous monitoring is broader (security AND functioning) than GDPR Art. 32(2) 5-risk-type detection. For DORA financial entities that are also controllers, the DORA Art. 8(1) / Art. 9(1) continuous monitoring discharges the financial entity's continuous-monitoring obligation and the GDPR detection capability becomes a sub-component within the DORA-led stack. **NIS 2 ↔ DORA EQUAL on continuous-monitoring dimension**: both regulations impose continuous-monitoring obligations (NIS 2 Art. 21(2)(b) detection capability vs. DORA Art. 8(1) per SR-DORA-030 OJ-literal locus Art. 9(1) `continuously monitor and control`); for DORA financial entities that are also NSIEs, the DORA Art. 9(1) continuous monitoring discharges both obligations — DORA-dominant; for non-financial NSIEs, NIS 2 is the authority. The proportionality factors per Art. 4 `in relation to the size, nature, scale and complexity of the financial entity's activities` calibrate the depth of the continuous monitoring + multi-layered detection + continuous ICT risk identification. **CRDA-flagged GENUINE TENSION with CRA (propagated)**: CRA Annex I Part I (2)(l) user opt-out vs. DORA Art. 8(1) mandatory continuous monitoring — Layer 2 OJ-level resolution required.

  priority: MUST
  verification_method: TEST
  dependencies: [10.1.1 (when DORA-controller — DORA Art. 8(1) / Art. 9(1) continuous monitoring is broader than GDPR Art. 32(2); DORA-led stack with GDPR detection as sub-component), 10.1.2 (when DORA-NSIE — **NIS 2 ↔ DORA EQUAL on continuous-monitoring dimension, DORA-dominant for financial entities**; DORA Art. 8(1) / Art. 9(1) continuous monitoring discharges both DORA's dedicated monitoring obligation and NIS 2's Art. 21(2)(b) partial-coverage detection dimension; for non-financial NSIEs, NIS 2 detection capability operates on the all-hazards basis without DORA), 10.1.3 (when DORA entity deploys CRA-regulated products — **CRDA-flagged GENUINE TENSION (propagated)**: CRA user opt-out vs. DORA mandatory monitoring; Layer 2 OJ-level resolution required), 10.1.5 (when DORA financial entity deploys high-risk AI — DORA continuous monitoring + AI Act post-market monitoring stack; DORA-led stack with AI Act as AI-system-level overlay)]

  nist_csf: [DE.CM-01, DE.CM-09, PR.PS-04, DE.AE-02, ID.RA-01, ID.RA-03]

  applicable_if:
    regs: [DORA]
    scope_overlap: Y   # DORA↔GDPR = Y (DORA financial entity = controller; DORA-led stack with GDPR 5-risk-type as sub-component); DORA↔NIS2 = Y (DORA financial entity = NSIE; **EQUAL on continuous-monitoring dimension, DORA-dominant for financial entities**); DORA↔CRA = Conditional (Y when DORA entity deploys CRA-regulated products; **CRDA-flagged GENUINE TENSION propagated** — CRA user opt-out vs. DORA mandatory monitoring); DORA↔AI_Act = Y (DORA financial entity deploys high-risk AI; DORA continuous monitoring + AI Act post-market monitoring stack); HL engages when the DORA sub-SO activates AND ≥1 other sub-SO activates on Y or Conditional-Y overlap.

  satisfaction:
    controls: []
    nodes: []

  considerations: |
    The DORA continuous-monitoring obligation at 10.1 is the **financial-entity continuous monitoring of ICT-system security and functioning** + **prompt anomalous-activity detection** + **continuous ICT risk identification** per Art. 8(1) per SR-DORA-030 OJ-literal locus Art. 9(1) + Art. 10(1) + Art. 10(2) + Art. 7(2). Art. 8(1) `Financial entities shall continuously monitor and control the security and functioning of ICT systems and tools and shall minimise the impact of ICT risk on ICT systems through the deployment of appropriate ICT security tools, policies and procedures` (per SR-DORA-030; OJ-literal locus Art. 9(1) per the SR clarification) — the `tools, policies and procedures` 3-way AND + the `continuously monitor and control` 2-verb AND + the `security and functioning` 2-AND are S2 VAG + POLY + COORD ambiguities. Art. 10(1) `promptly detect anomalous activities` (per SR-DORA-011) + Art. 10(2) multiple layers + alert thresholds + auto-alert to relevant staff (per SR-DORA-012). Art. 7(2) continuous ICT risk identification on a continuous basis + yearly review (per SR-DORA-004). The recipient is the **NCA + management body** on the Art. 8(1) continuous monitoring + Art. 10(1)+(2) multi-layered detection + Art. 7(2) continuous ICT risk identification. **DORA-led stack for financial entities** — for DORA financial entities that are also controllers, the DORA Art. 8(1) / Art. 9(1) continuous monitoring discharges the financial entity's continuous-monitoring obligation and the GDPR detection capability becomes a sub-component within the DORA-led stack. **NIS 2 ↔ DORA EQUAL on continuous-monitoring dimension** — DORA-dominant for financial entities. **CRDA-flagged GENUINE TENSION with CRA (propagated)**: CRA user opt-out vs. DORA mandatory monitoring — Layer 2 OJ-level resolution required. Proportionality factors per Art. 4 `in relation to the size, nature, scale and complexity of the financial entity's activities` calibrate the depth.
```

#### 10.1.5 — AI-system post-market monitoring `proportionate to the nature of the AI technologies and the risks` + analysis-to-risk-management feedback loop

```yaml
- req_id: 10.1.5
  volere_section: 15
  volere_subtype: audit

  title: "Provider of high-risk AI system establishes and documents a post-market monitoring system proportionate to the nature of the AI technologies and the risks, actively and systematically collecting, documenting and analysing relevant data across the AI lifecycle"

  derives_from:
    hso_sub_so: SO-10.1.AI_Act
    per_reg_so: SO-AIACT-012
    source_sr: SR-AIACT-021 + SR-AIACT-022
    hso_file: ../../HierarchicalSecurityObjectives/D-10_Monitoring-Audit/10.1.md
    objective_ref_section: "### 10.1.5"

  description: |
    The product shall establish and document a post-market monitoring system in a manner that is proportionate to the nature of the AI technologies and the risks of the high-risk AI system (per AI Act Art. 72(1) `Providers shall establish and document a post-market monitoring system in a manner that is proportionate to the nature of the AI technologies and the risks of the high-risk AI system`), with the system actively and systematically collecting, documenting and analysing relevant data (per Art. 72(2) `The post-market monitoring system shall actively and systematically collect, document and analyse relevant data`), and market-surveillance cooperation under Regulation (EU) 2019/1020 applying (per Art. 74(1)).

  fit_criterion: |
    A post-market monitoring system is established and documented per Art. 72(1) + Art. 72(2) + Art. 74(1) (SR-AIACT-021 + SR-AIACT-022) verified by DE.CM-09 primary (Computing hardware and software, runtime environments, and their data are monitored — anchors the AI Act Art. 72(2) `actively and systematically collect, document and analyse relevant data` mapped to NIST CSF 2.0 DE.CM-09) + ID.IM-04 (Adequate resource capacity to ensure availability is maintained — anchors the post-market monitoring system per DomainAnalysis cross-validation) + GV.OV-03 (Cybersecurity risk management strategy outcomes are reviewed and inform changes — anchors the Art. 72 analysis-to-risk-management feedback loop); the post-market monitoring system is documented in a manner that is `proportionate to the nature of the AI technologies and the risks` (qualitative proportionality — per SR-AIACT-021 ambiguity_notes, the OJ does not quantify the proportionality; the chosen reading is: a documented proportionality assessment that calibrates the depth of monitoring to the AI technology class and risk category); the Art. 72(2) `actively and systematically collect, document and analyse` 3-way verb AND is operational across the AI-system lifecycle (collection channels + documentation layer + analysis layer + feedback loop); the analysis-to-risk-management feedback loop feeds back into the provider's risk-management-system documentation (Art. 9 — cross to 9.1 AI Act sub-SO) and the AI-system quality management system (Art. 17 — cross to 9.1 AI Act sub-SO); the Art. 72(3) post-market monitoring plan is documented and operational; the Art. 74(1) market-surveillance cooperation under Regulation (EU) 2019/1020 provides the MSA + AI Office recipient channel; the recipient is the **MSA (market surveillance authority) + AI Office + provider** on the Art. 72(1)-(3) post-market monitoring + Art. 74(1) market-surveillance cooperation; verified by MSA + AI Office audit pack (post-market monitoring system documentation, Art. 72 proportionality assessment, Art. 72(2) 3-way verb AND evidence, Art. 72(3) post-market monitoring plan, analysis-to-risk-management feedback loop evidence, Art. 74(1) market-surveillance cooperation evidence) + automated Art. 72(2) 3-way verb AND test (collection channels operational + documentation layer operational + analysis layer operational) + automated analysis-to-risk-management feedback loop test (post-market monitoring analysis feeds back into Art. 9 risk-management-system documentation + Art. 17 QMS). **IMPORTANT — AI Act post-market monitoring feedback loop** (Art. 72(2) `actively and systematically collect, document and analyse` → analysis-to-risk-management loop feeding Art. 9 + Art. 17) is AI-Act-specific and is preserved in this sub-SO. The AI Act Art. 72 monitoring is provider-side and is NOT subject to user opt-out (unlike CRA Annex I Part I (2)(l) opt-out); the CRA opt-out tension does NOT extend to the AI Act pair.

  priority: MUST
  verification_method: TEST
  dependencies: [10.1.1 (when provider/deployer = controller — GDPR pre-breach detection + AI Act lifecycle monitoring on different trigger alignment; pre-breach vs. lifecycle; the two regimes stack without overlap), 10.1.2 (when NSIE deploys high-risk AI — NIS 2 entity-level operational monitoring + AI Act AI-system lifecycle monitoring; different trigger alignment; the two regimes stack without overlap), 10.1.3 (when CRA-regulated product is also high-risk AI system — CRA Annex I conformity + AI Act post-market monitoring stack for AI-enabled products; the AI Act adds the feedback-loop dimension; the CRA opt-out tension does NOT extend to the AI Act pair — the AI Act Art. 72 monitoring is provider-side, not subject to user opt-out), 10.1.4 (when DORA financial entity deploys high-risk AI — DORA continuous monitoring + AI Act post-market monitoring stack; DORA-led stack with AI Act as AI-system-level overlay; the two regimes stack without overlap)]

  nist_csf: [DE.CM-09, ID.IM-04, GV.OV-03]

  applicable_if:
    regs: [AI_Act]
    scope_overlap: Conditional   # AI_Act↔GDPR = Conditional (Y when high-risk AI processes personal data — provider is controller; pre-breach vs. lifecycle; different trigger alignment); AI_Act↔NIS2 = Conditional (Y when NSIE deploys high-risk AI; entity-level operational vs. AI-system lifecycle); AI_Act↔CRA = Conditional (Y when CRA-regulated product is also high-risk AI; CRA Annex I conformity + AI Act Art. 72 post-market monitoring stack; AI Act adds feedback-loop dimension; the CRA opt-out tension does NOT extend to the AI Act pair); AI_Act↔DORA = Y (DORA financial entity deploys high-risk AI; DORA continuous monitoring + AI Act post-market monitoring stack; DORA-led stack with AI Act as AI-system-level overlay); HL engages when the AI Act sub-SO activates AND ≥1 other sub-SO activates on Y or Conditional-Y overlap.

  satisfaction:
    controls: []
    nodes: []

  considerations: |
    The AI Act continuous-monitoring obligation at 10.1 is the **AI-system post-market monitoring system** per Art. 72(1) + Art. 72(2) + Art. 72(3) + Art. 74(1). Art. 72(1) `Providers shall establish and document a post-market monitoring system in a manner that is proportionate to the nature of the AI technologies and the risks of the high-risk AI system` (per SR-AIACT-021) — the `proportionate to the nature of the AI technologies and the risks` proportionality is qualitative (S3 VAG per SR-AIACT-021 ambiguity_notes — OJ does not quantify the proportionality; chosen reading: documented proportionality assessment calibrating depth to AI technology class + risk category). Art. 72(2) `The post-market monitoring system shall actively and systematically collect, document and analyse relevant data` (per SR-AIACT-022) — the 3-way verb AND `actively and systematically collect, document and analyse` is S2 COORD (per SR-AIACT-022 ambiguity_notes). Art. 72(3) post-market monitoring plan; Art. 74(1) market-surveillance cooperation under Regulation (EU) 2019/1020. The analysis-to-risk-management feedback loop feeds back into the provider's risk-management-system documentation (Art. 9 — cross to 9.1 AI Act sub-SO) and the AI-system quality management system (Art. 17 — cross to 9.1 AI Act sub-SO). The recipient is the **MSA + AI Office + provider** on the Art. 72(1)-(3) post-market monitoring + Art. 74(1) market-surveillance cooperation. The AI Act Art. 72 monitoring is provider-side and is **NOT subject to user opt-out** (unlike CRA Annex I Part I (2)(l) opt-out); the CRA opt-out tension does NOT extend to the AI Act pair.
```

# Part 4 — Relevant Ambiguity (by regulation)

## GDPR

##### Art. 10 — Processing of criminal convictions

**Clause: GDPR-CL25** | type: principle (criminal data) | obligatedParty: CONTROLLER (official authority only for comprehensive registers) | obligationType: PER-RECORD

**Berry anchor:** §5.1 (`appropriate safeguards`, `comprehensive register`).

**Instance 1 — VAG / S3 / `comprehensive register`**

`comprehensive register` is undefined; the term encompasses national criminal-record databases but the boundary with sectoral registers (e.g. fraud registers, sanctions lists, AML/KYC databases) is contested.

**Variant readings:**
| # | Reading | Disambiguation source |
|---|---|---|
| R1 | `Comprehensive register` = national criminal-records database (e.g. UK's Police National Computer). | Recital 50 (non-binding); Member State practice. |
| R2 | `Comprehensive register` = any database covering all convictions of a population segment (including fraud registers, sex-offender registers). | EDPB Guidelines on criminal-conviction data. |
| R3 | `Comprehensive register` = any database whose coverage exceeds sectoral purpose. | Loose reading. |

R1 vs R3 yield materially distinct scope of `official authority` control.

### Article 11 — Processing not requiring identification

#### Verbatim (with highlighting)

> **Article 11**
>
> 1. The controller shall not be obliged to maintain, acquire or process **additional information** to identify the data subject for the sole purpose of complying with this Regulation in order to comply with the obligations laid down in Articles 5 to 9 (...).

> 2. Where in cases referred to in this Article the controller is able to demonstrate that it is not in a position to identify the data subject, the controller shall inform the data subject accordingly, if possible. In such cases, Articles 15 to 20 do not apply except where the data subject, for the purpose of exercising his or her rights under those articles, provides **additional information** enabling his or her identification.

#### Clause breakdown

##### Art. 21(1) — Objection grounds and overriding

**Clause: GDPR-RT16** | type: data subject right (object) | obligatedParty: CONTROLLER | obligationType: PER-OBJECTION

**Berry anchor:** §5.1 (`compelling legitimate grounds`).

**Instance 1 — VAG / S3 / `compelling legitimate grounds`**

`compelling` is stronger than Art. 6(1)(f) `legitimate interests`. The `overriding` test for compelling grounds is undefined.

**Variant readings:**
| # | Reading | Disambiguation source |
|---|---|---|
| R1 | `Compelling` = strictly necessary (lex specialis to legitimate interests). | EDPB Guidelines on legitimate interests. |
| R2 | `Compelling` = materially more weighty than legitimate interests. | Loose reading. |

R1 vs R2 yield materially distinct balance-tests in objection handling.

---

##### Art. 28(3) — Processor contract content (8 mandatory clauses)

**Clause: GDPR-CP09** | type: processor-side obligation | obligatedParty: PROCESSOR | obligationType: PER-CONTRACT

**Berry anchor:** §5.4.7 (8-element closed AND-list (a)–(h)).

**Instance 1 — COORD / S2 / 8-element AND-list**

Each sub-clause is mandatory. S2 because all 8 sub-clauses are substantive obligations with the same compliance practice.

---

##### Art. 32(1) — Security measures (open list)

**Clause: GDPR-CP15** | type: security | obligatedParty: CONTROLLER + PROCESSOR | obligationType: CONTINUOUS

**Berry anchor:** §5.1 (`state of the art`, `appropriate`, `as appropriate`), §5.4.7 (4-element list), §3.3.1 (CIA+R polysemy).

**Instance 1 — VAG / S3 / `state of the art`**

Cross-clause propagation — same as Art. 25(1). S3 because security-engineering scope is material to compliance.

**Instance 2 — COORD / S3 / `(a) AND (b) AND (c) AND (d)`**

The 4-element list `(a) pseudonymisation/encryption, (b) CIA+R, (c) restore-in-timely-manner, (d) regular-testing` is closed (and). All 4 required.

**Instance 3 — POLY+VAG / S3 / `in a timely manner` (Art. 32(1)(c))**

`Timely manner` is the textbook Berry-flagged temporal vague phrase. Recital 49 (non-binding) attempts to fix: "restore the availability and access as soon as possible". The cross-reference is non-binding and the operational meaning drifts.

**Variant readings:**
| # | Reading | Disambiguation source |
|---|---|---|
| R1 | `Timely` = RTO defined per processing activity (objective measure). | Industry standard (BCP/DRP best practice). |
| R2 | `Timely` = as-soon-as-practicable for the specific incident. | EDPB Guidelines 7/2019 (generic). |

R1 vs R2 yield materially distinct BCP/DRP engineering scope.

---

##### Art. 32(2) — Risk-enumeration for security assessment

**Clause: GDPR-CP16** | type: security risk assessment | obligatedParty: CONTROLLER + PROCESSOR | obligationType: CONTINUOUS

**Berry anchor:** §5.4.7 (`accidental or unlawful destruction, loss, alteration, unauthorised disclosure of, or access to` — 5-way OR chain).

**Instance 1 — COORD / S3 / 5-way OR**

The 5-event enumeration (Berry §5.4.7 menu-card pattern). Each OR is inclusive; the OR-chain admits ≥5 distinct threat-model scopes.

**Variant readings:**
| # | Reading | Disambiguation source |
|---|---|---|
| R1 | Any of the 5 events triggers the security assessment obligation. | Strict reading. |
| R2 | The 5 events are illustrative; controller determines in-scope events. | Loose reading. |

R1 vs R2 yield materially distinct threat-modelling scope.

### Article 33 — Breach notification to supervisory authority

#### Verbatim (with highlighting)

> **Article 33**
> **Notification of a personal data breach to the supervisory authority**
>
> 1. In the case of a personal data breach, the controller shall **without undue delay** and, **where feasible**, **not later than 72 hours after having become aware of it**, notify the personal data breach to the supervisory authority competent in accordance with Article 55, unless the personal data breach is **unlikely to result in a risk** to the rights and freedoms of natural persons. Where the notification to the supervisory authority is not made within 72 hours, it shall be accompanied by reasons for the delay.

> 2. The processor shall notify the controller **without undue delay** after becoming aware of a personal data breach.

> 3. The notification referred to in paragraph 1 shall at least:

> (a) describe the nature of the personal data breach including **where possible**, the categories and **approximate number** of data subjects concerned and the categories and approximate number of personal data records concerned;

> (b) communicate the name and contact details of the data protection officer or other contact point where more information can be obtained;

> (c) describe the **likely consequences** of the personal data breach;

> (d) describe the measures taken or proposed to be taken by the controller to address the personal data breach, including, where appropriate, measures to mitigate its possible adverse effects.

#### Clause breakdown

##### Art. 33(1) — 72-hour notification

**Clause: GDPR-CP17** | type: breach notification | obligatedParty: CONTROLLER | obligationType: TRIGGERED (per breach)

**Berry anchor:** §5.1 (`without undue delay`, `where feasible`, `unlikely`), §5.7 (temporal operators), §3.3.1 (`becoming aware` polysemy).

**Instance 1 — VAG / S3 / `without undue delay and, where feasible, not later than 72 hours`**

Twin temporal operators (Berry §5.1, §5.7) — `without undue delay` is soft, `72 hours` is hard, `where feasible` softens the hard. Interaction undefined.

**Instance 2 — SCOPE-Q / S3 / `becoming aware of it`**

The awareness trigger is polysemous (cf. CRA C25, NIS 2 C25 — same pattern across regulations).

**Instance 3 — POLY+VAG / S3 / `unlikely to result in a risk`**

`Unlikely` is qualitative; `risk` is the recurring polysemy (multiple threshold definitions across GDPR).

**Variant readings:**
| # | Reading | Disambiguation source |
|---|---|---|
| R1 | Notification required unless certainty-of-no-risk; precautionary principle. | EDPB Guidelines 9/2022 on breach notification. |
| R2 | Notification required only when risk threshold is met; controller assessment suffices. | Industry reading. |

R1 vs R2 yield materially distinct breach-response workflows.

#### 3.12 CRA-CL13 — Art. 8(2) — Annex IV amendment

### 4. Cross-clause structural observations (Art. 6–8)

1. **Art. 6 is the essential-requirements gate.** The two-part structure (Part I for product properties; Part II for processes) mirrors GDPR's two-tier obligation (Art. 25 design + Art. 32 security). The installation-condition proviso (CL02) is CRA-unique and operationally consequential: a non-conforming product that is "properly installed" satisfies Art. 6(a).

2. **Art. 7's two criteria (CL06, CL07) define important products.** The criteria are disjunctive (criterion A OR B). Three materially distinct readings admit materially different compliance populations. The Commission's 11 December 2025 implementing acts (CL09) will refine the criteria.

3. **Art. 8's certification requirement (CL10) is the most consequential single clause in this article.** Critical products require European cybersecurity certification at assurance level "substantial." As of mid-2025, **no European cybersecurity certification scheme has been adopted under the CSA that covers critical-product categories.** The fallback (CL12) routes products to Art. 32(3) conformity assessment (modules B+C or H).

4. **The Art. 7/8/Alex III/IV architecture is a 3-tier classification** (analogous to AI Act's risk tiers):
   - Default (Art. 6): all products meet Annex I (essential requirements) — Annex I compliance route.
   - Important (Art. 7): Class I or Class II per Annex III — Art. 32(2)/(3) conformity assessment.
   - Critical (Art. 8): Annex IV — European cybersecurity certification at substantial level (or Art. 32(3) fallback).

   Each tier imposes progressively stricter conformity-assessment obligations.

5. **`core functionality` (CL04) is the single most consequential classification term.** The reading determines which products fall into Class I/II vs default. R1–R3 admit materially different compliance populations.

6. **Annex III/IV are flagged for Layer 2.** Their ambiguity is definitional regress ("core functionality of a product category") rather than Berry linguistic in the strict sense.

---

## CRA v0.2 — Annex I Essential Cybersecurity Requirements

> **Source.** OJ L, 20.11.2024, p. 68–69. `03_REFERENCE_MATERIAL/Regulatory_References/CRA.txt`, lines ~4153–4234.
>
> **Pattern.** Each Part quoted verbatim with highlighting, then per-clause analysis. Universal `[recital NN]` convention.
>
> **Annex I is the binding normative core of CRA.** Part I defines security properties for products; Part II defines vulnerability-handling obligations for manufacturers. These are referenced by Art. 6, 7, 8, 13, 27, 28, 32, and are the ultimate compliance target. 21 of the 26 T2 clauses lived in Annex I.

---

## NIS2

#### 3.1 NIS2-CL07 — Art. 21(1) sentence 1 — Risk-management core obligation

**Source locus:**

> Member States shall ensure that essential and important entities take **<mark>appropriate and proportionate technical, operational and organisational measures</mark>** to manage the risks posed to the security of network and information systems which those entities use for their operations or for the provision of their services, and to prevent or minimise the **impact** of incidents on recipients of their services and on other services.

**Instance 1 — VAG — S3 — `appropriate and proportionate`**

The opening qualifier. Three materially distinct readings:

- **R1:** `appropriate` and `proportionate` are independent (entity must satisfy both tests, separately).
- **R2:** `appropriate` modifies `proportionate` (entity must satisfy proportionality, with `appropriate` as a placeholder synonym).
- **R3:** `appropriate and proportionate` is a single compound qualifier (a fixed phrase).

| # | Reading | Disambiguation source |
|---|---|---|
| R1 | Independent | None in OJ |
| R2 | Hierarchical | None in OJ |
| R3 | Compound | In-Directive: Art. 21(1) sentence 2 operationalises proportionality by specific factors (exposure, size, likelihood, severity) |

R1 and R3 produce materially distinct compliance obligations (R1 admits two separate failure modes; R3 admits one composite test). S3.

This is the dominant VAG trigger of NIS 2. **It propagates to all 10 sub-points of Art. 21(2)(a)–(j) and to Art. 21(3) and Art. 21(4).** Every measure listed in Art. 21(2) inherits the `appropriate and proportionate` qualifier by reference to "The measures referred to in paragraph 1".

*Berry anchor:* §5.1 (`appropriate`, `proportionate`).

**Instance 2 — POLY — S2 — `technical, operational and organisational measures`**

Three-term coordination. Three readings:

- **R1:** Three distinct categories of measures (entity must implement at least one of each).
- **R2:** Cumulative description (a measure is "technical, operational and organisational" if it has all three characteristics).
- **R3:** Open list (entity may add more categories).

| # | Reading | Disambiguation source |
|---|---|---|
| R1 | Distinct categories | In-Directive: literal `technical, operational and organisational` |
| R2 | Cumulative single measure | None in OJ |
| R3 | Open list | In-Directive: recital 56 (proportionality) |

R1 is the literal reading. R3 is the dominant reading (recital 56 implies entity-discretion on additional categories). S2.

*Berry anchor:* §5.5 (three-way coordination).

**Instance 3 — VAG + POLY — S2 — `manage the risks`**

`manage the risks` — what counts as `management`? Reading:

- **R1:** Identify + assess + mitigate.
- **R2:** Treat (avoid, accept, transfer, mitigate).
- **R3:** Continuous monitoring + adjustment.

| # | Reading | Disambiguation source |
|---|---|---|
| R1 | Identify-assess-mitigate | None in OJ; ISO/IEC 27005 |
| R2 | ISO 27005 risk-treatment | None in OJ; ISO/IEC 27005 |
| R3 | Continuous monitoring | None in OJ; NIST RMF |

*Berry anchor:* §3.3.1 (`manage`).

**Instance 4 — POLY — S2 — `impact`**

`impact` — on recipients of services (downstream effect) or on other services (cascading effect)?

- **R1:** Impact on direct recipients.
- **R2:** Impact on other services (cascade).
- **R3:** Both.

| # | Reading | Disambiguation source |
|---|---|---|
| R1 | Recipients | In-Directive: literal `recipients of their services` |
| R2 | Other services | In-Directive: literal `on other services` |
| R3 | Both | In-Directive: literal `recipients … and … other services` |

R3 is the literal reading (coordinated by `and`). The fact that the two objects are coordinated by AND rather than OR means the entity must consider impact on both. S2 because the surface ambiguity is between "focus on direct recipients" and "consider cascading effects".

#### 3.3 NIS2-CL09 — Art. 21(2) chapeau — All-hazards approach + physical environment

**Source locus:**

> The measures referred to in paragraph 1 shall be **based on an all-hazards approach** that aims to protect network and information systems and **<mark>the physical environment of those systems</mark>** from incidents…

**Instance 1 — VAG — S2 — `all-hazards approach`**

`all-hazards` — natural, technological, malicious, accidental? Cybersecurity only or broader?

- **R1:** All cybersecurity hazards (intentional, accidental, environmental).
- **R2:** All hazards including natural disasters (earthquake, flood), pandemics.
- **R3:** All hazards including physical security (intrusion, theft, sabotage).

| # | Reading | Disambiguation source |

| R1 | Cybersecurity-only | None in OJ |
| R2 | Including natural | None in OJ |
| R3 | Including physical | In-Directive: literal `physical environment of those systems` |

R3 is the dominant reading (the chapeau explicitly includes physical-environment protection). S2.

*Berry anchor:* §5.1 (`all-hazards`).

**Instance 2 — VAG + SCOPE-Q — S2 — `the physical environment of those systems`**

The physical environment is in scope. The reading:

- **R1:** Server rooms, data centres, network equipment closets.
- **R2:** Buildings (offices, call centres).
- **R3:** Surrounding critical infrastructure (power, cooling, telecoms).

| # | Reading | Disambiguation source |
|---|---|---|
| R1 | Data-centre scope | In-Directive: literal `physical environment` |
| R2 | Building scope | None in OJ |
| R3 | Critical-infrastructure scope | None in OJ |

R1 is the literal reading. R2 and R3 are operational extensions. S2.

**Layer 1 observation** — The physical-environment scope of NIS 2 is broader than DORA, CRA, AI Act (all of which are software/cyber-focused). The all-hazards approach with physical-environment coverage is the **single most distinctive feature of NIS 2 vs the other four regulations** in the corpus. **For software-compliance analysis, the physical-environment dimension is out-of-scope**: it does not generate software requirements, only physical-security requirements. The clause is included for completeness but flagged as **out-of-scope for the software-compliance track of the methodology**. Layer 3 (inference-trust) will need to adjudicate whether software-software integration controls are needed for the physical-environment dimension (e.g. environmental sensors feeding into SIEM).

*Berry anchor:* §5.1 (`physical environment`); §5.2.1 (scope).

---

#### 3.5 NIS2-CL11 — Art. 21(2)(b) — Incident handling

**Source locus:**

> **(b)** incident handling;

**Instance 1 — POLY — S2 — `incident handling`**

The phrase is defined in Art. 6(8): `any actions and procedures aiming to prevent, detect, analyse, and contain or to respond to and recover from an incident`. The 6-action list is illustrative (`aiming to`).

- **R1:** The 6-action list is exhaustive.
- **R2:** The 6-action list is a floor; entity must add more actions.
- **R3:** `incident handling` is process-only (NIST SP 800-61 model).
- **R4:** `incident handling` is team-only (CSIRT/SOC personnel).

| # | Reading | Disambiguation source |
|---|---|---|
| R1 | Exhaustive | None in OJ |
| R2 | Floor | In-Directive: `aiming to` (illustrative) |
| R3 | Process | None in OJ; NIST SP 800-61 |
| R4 | Team | None in OJ; ENISA CSIRT-services guidance |

R2 is the literal reading. R3 vs R4 is a Layer 1 polysemy. S2.

*Berry anchor:* §3.3.1 (`incident handling`); §5.1 (`aiming to`).

## DORA

## DORA v0.2 — Chapter II Section I + Art. 4: Governance and Proportionality (Art. 4–6)

> **Source.** OJ L 333, 27.12.2022, p. 29–32. `03_REFERENCE_MATERIAL/Regulatory_References/DORA.txt`, lines **1635–1789**.
>
> **Pattern.** Each article quoted in full, then per-clause analysis. Universal `[recital NN]` convention. DORA-specific fields: `definitional-anchor`, `proportionality-status: Art. 4 applies`, `RTS-pending`, `scope`.
>
> **Why these three articles together (umbrella file `02a`).** Art. 4 is in Ch. I (General Provisions) but the proportionality principle it establishes **propagates to every clause in Ch. II–V Sec. I**. Art. 5 (governance) and Art. 6 (framework chapeau) are the structural anchors to which Art. 4 attaches. Grouping them in `02a` lets the reader see the **proportionality overlay + management-body liability + framework chapeau** as a single conceptual block. The remaining 10 articles of Ch. II Sec. II (Art. 7–16, the operational risk-management provisions) are analysed in `02b_DORA_Ch2Sec2_Framework.md`.
>
> **Articles in this file:** 3 articles, 17 paragraphs (Art. 4: 3; Art. 5: 4 + 9 sub-points; Art. 6: 10 + 8 sub-points), 13 atomic clauses (CL4-1 … CL6-5).

---

### 1. Articles covered

| Article | Title | Paragraphs | T3 clause | New in v0.2? |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Art. 4 | Proportionality principle | 3 | **C01** (proportionality) | No (extended — T3 had a single clause; v0.2 has 3) |
| Art. 5 | Governance and organisation | 4 + 9 sub-points (a)–(i) | **C02** (governance), **C03** (management body), **C04** (board training) | No (extended — T3 had 3 clauses; v0.2 has 5) |
| Art. 6 | ICT risk management framework (chapeau) | 10 + 8 sub-points (a)–(h) | **C05** (framework chapeau), **C06** (control function), **C07** (audit), **C08** (resilience strategy) | No (extended — T3 had 4 clauses; v0.2 has 5) |

**Note on Art. 4 (Proportionality):** the three paragraphs of Art. 4 attach proportionality to (i) Ch. II as the default; (ii) Ch. III, IV, V Sec. I by specific reference; (iii) competent-authority review under Art. 6(5) and Art. 16(2). Each clause card in the rest of v0.2 carries a `proportionality-status: Art. 4 applies` flag (per pilot convention).

**Note on definitional anchors:** every ICT-related clause in this file inherits from DORA Art. 3(2) → NIS 2 Art. 6(1) `network and information system`; Art. 3(5) `ICT risk`; Art. 3(30) `management body` (composite cross-reference, see pilot file §2.10). The Art. 3(22) `critical or important function` (S3 tautology, see pilot file §2.7) propagates to Art. 6(8)(f).

**Note on RTS-pending:** no RTS mandate in this article range. Art. 15 (RTS mandate to ESAs on Art. 9–11 elements) is meta-procedural; the substantive impact lives in Art. 9–11, not in this file. The Art. 4 proportionality principle is **self-implementing** — no RTS/ITS specifies the criteria.
